# C. <comment_start id=kix.vjhl65yq85f4><comment_start id=kix.zigt3l9314yp>OVERVIEW<comment_end id=kix.vjhl65yq85f4>
<comment_end id=kix.zigt3l9314yp>Fresno County faces a growing public health crisis from extreme heat, with 64 days in 2024 exceeding 100°F. On October 3, 2024, the county recorded a scorching 105°F—the highest October temperature in Fresno's history. Climate projections indicate this trend will worsen, with annual average maximum temperatures rising by 5.3°F to 8.7°F by the end of the century. The number of “extreme heat days,” defined as those surpassing 94.3°F, is expected to increase from the historical average of five per year to as many as 66 under high emissions scenarios.
These rising temperatures exacerbate existing disparities. Over 55% of Fresno County census tracts rank above the 75th percentile on CalEnviroScreen 4.0, reflecting burdens such as poverty, air pollution, and health inequities like asthma and cardiovascular diseases. Many residents in low-income communities lack access to cooling resources, such as air conditioning, shaded pedestrian routes, or energy-efficient housing, leaving them disproportionately exposed to heat-related illnesses.
However, in Fresno County census tracts are typically spatially large enough that their attributes tell us little about the relationship between built environment parameters such as shade from trees, and heat stress impacts. Disadvantaged communities,<comment_start id=kix.b3r1oqhf45x7> particularly in South Fresno,<comment_end id=kix.b3r1oqhf45x7> suffer from shade inequity; r<comment_start id=kix.wwgrm56loy8y>esearch using high resolution geospatial LiDAR data and metrics like Mean Radiant Temperature (MRT) reveals that low-income neighborhoods experience MRT levels as high as 122.7°F, compared to 115.7°F<comment_end id=kix.wwgrm56loy8y> in wealthier areas. This disparity intensifies health risks for agricultural workers, who endure prolonged exposure to extreme heat with minimal shade.
To address these inequities, our Fresno County Extreme Heat Analysis will combine advanced heat vulnerability assessment with robust community engagement. Using high resolution data from ERA5 satellite, ECOSTRESS satellite imagery, Google StreetView, aerial surveys, LiDAR and other geospatial datasets, we will analyze both urban and rural heat island effects, tree canopy coverage, and socioeconomic indicators to identify vulnerable hotspots. These areas of interest will then be taken back to the Working Group and wider community to select the five Project Areas designated for detailed planning and analysis as specified by the RFP. 
To ensure a comprehensive understanding of heat as a lived experience, and actual heat stress, we also employ advanced metrics such as the Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI), which incorporate factors like wind flow, radiant temperature, and land use in addition to traditional heat and humidity indicators. This approach captures the multifaceted nature of thermal stress, providing a nuanced view of how environmental conditions affect daily life. By integrating these metrics, our analysis moves beyond static temperature readings to develop interventions that reflect the complexity of local microclimates and the lived realities of Fresno County's most vulnerable populations.
Our methodology integrates technical rigor with community insights through multilingual outreach, interactive workshops, and iterative feedback. This process ensures localized strategies for increasing tree canopy and shade access are informed by those most affected. Working collaboratively with Fresno COG, local jurisdictions, and community organizations, our project will deliver actionable, evidence based plans to mitigate extreme heat impacts and promote environmental justice. By prioritizing interventions in Fresno County’s most vulnerable communities, this analysis will also address broader social and health inequities, advancing long term resilience for all residents.
Our project team brings together experts in greening interventions, climate analysis, community engagement, infrastructure planning and urban forestry to deliver a comprehensive extreme heat analysis and tree canopy plan. The project will be led by our core team of inhouse landscape designers, arborist, climate scientists, urban planners, GIS and data visualization experts, civil engineers. 
We are also bringing to the table our collaborator and partner Trees Fresno, local community engagement specialists, contractors’ estimators and a network existing of local EJ, and government relations from other projects in the area. We will also coordinate:
  - Fresno COG Project Manager
  - Working Group representatives from local jurisdictions and community organizations
  - Technical advisors specializing in heat vulnerability assessment and urban forestry
  - Community engagement specialists focusing on disadvantaged communities
The project will follow a quarterly timeline. Each quarter aligns with a round of deliverables.
# D. Detailed Work Plan
## <comment_start id=kix.kmvft11vqxox><comment_start id=kix.mfmbktbb7ejk>Task 1: Collaboration <comment_end id=kix.kmvft11vqxox><comment_end id=kix.mfmbktbb7ejk>and Community Engagement
### **Approach**
This task establishes an Extreme Heat Working Group, develops a comprehensive Public Outreach Plan, and executes community engagement activities. Hyphae Design Lab will lead the coordination effort, in close collaboration with Binational of Central California (BOCC), Urban Diversity Design (UDD) and Tree Fresno (TF), ensuring equity-driven, culturally informed participation across Fresno County.
<comment_start id=kix.jdzi239po3dl>Hyphae will coordinate and convene the Working Group, in close collaboration with BOCC and with targeted input from <comment_end id=kix.jdzi239po3dl>Sheila Hakimipour at UDD and Tree Fresno and their combined <comment_start id=kix.jdzi239po3dl>institutional knowledge of Fresno County. <comment_end id=kix.jdzi239po3dl>Simultaneously, BOCC will begin developing the public outreach plan, embedding the community outreach, community mapping, and surveying components of this project into their existing programming. Leveraging their deep ties within Fresno County’s underserved communities, BOCC will conduct outreach and engagement activities at their culturally responsive events, such as resource fairs, tax preparation sessions, Medi-Cal enrollment, as well as health and wellness screenings. They will also facilitate public engagement through surveys, listening sessions and focus groups, ensuring broad community participation and meaningful feedback.
Hyphae Design Lab will provide technical expertise and project oversight, integrating heat vulnerability analysis into the overall project framework and facilitating collaboration between communities, state agencies, municipalities, and regulators. Brent Bucknum and Ivan Heitmann will oversee the development of the initial work plan, budget, and schedule, and lead quarterly meetings to adjust timelines and strategies. Hyphae will also ensure seamless integration of geospatial analysis into outreach efforts, connecting community input to actionable design strategies.
Tree Fresno, under the leadership of Mona Cummings, will contribute their extensive local knowledge and network to support outreach efforts. Their role will include identifying high-priority neighborhoods for urban greening interventions and engaging with the community to answer technical questions about tree planting and urban forestry. Tree Fresno will collaborate with Hyphae to develop greening strategies that align with community input and technical analysis.
#### Formation of the Extreme Heat Working Group
**Process:**
  - The team–Hyphae, UDD, BOCC, and Tree Fresno–in collaboration with COG, will first pool their contacts to identify and recruit the key stakeholders making up the Working Group during the first three months of programming (February to April 2025). Partners will use their established networks to identify representatives from disadvantaged communities, ensuring meaningful participation.
  - The Working Group will include representatives from the City of Fresno, eastside and westside cities, Fresno County, the Fresno County Department of Public Health, active transportation organizations, and community-based/nonprofit organizations.
  - Some other potential candidates include local health-based organizations already working to remediate extreme heat. These include the 
      - **West Fresno Family Resource Center**: A trusted grassroots organization providing culturally and linguistically effective health improvement and illness prevention programs to underserved communities in Southwest Fresno.
      - **Fresno Building Healthy Communities**: A coalition dedicated to fostering equity through civic engagement, youth leadership, and community-driven advocacy for better health, housing, and environmental justice.
      - **Fresno County Department of Public Health Office of Emergency Services (OES)**: The county’s lead agency for disaster preparedness and emergency response, including initiatives addressing extreme weather events.
      - **Central California Asthma Collaborative**: Experts in air quality and respiratory health, providing data-driven interventions and community education to mitigate the compounding health impacts of extreme heat and poor air quality.
      - **Fresno Economic Opportunities Commission (Fresno EOC)**: Notably through their "Cooling with Care" initiative, they provide resources and assistance to vulnerable populations during extreme heat events.
      - **Fresno Mission**: Serving unhoused populations, the Fresno Mission provides critical shelter and resources, making them a valuable partner for addressing heat risks among the city’s most vulnerable residents.
**<comment_start id=kix.h3zx6m4qnah8>Structure and Schedule (6 Quarterly Working Group Meetings):<comment_end id=kix.h3zx6m4qnah8>**
  - The Working Group will meet quarterly, starting in May 2025, for **five total meetings** (3 hours each). These meetings will guide project analyses, provide feedback on outreach efforts, and inform the development of proposed projects. This collaborative structure ensures that the Working Group plays an active role in shaping the project’s direction at each phase.
  - At six months (Q2), the Working Group will select 20-30 initial hotspot areas based on vulnerability analysis, data insights, and community input. This list will then be presented to the broader community for feedback during a Zoom meeting facilitated by Hyphae and BOCC, with translation services provided to ensure accessibility for diverse linguistic groups.
  - After incorporating community feedback, the Working Group will narrow the list to five cities (Q4). To refine these selections further, Hyphae will organize a second community Zoom meeting with BOCC’s assistance. This meeting will gather additional localized insights and confirm alignment with community priorities.
  - Over the remaining programming period, BOCC will organize five community events in the selected areas, embedding the project’s surveying and data collection efforts within BOCC’s existing programming in these regions. This approach minimizes disruption while leveraging BOCC’s established networks and culturally resonant activities, ensuring robust participation and meaningful community engagement.
  - Sheila Hakimipour will bring valuable local contact with local engineers and city officials in reviewing existing plans, policies, and guidelines regarding climate adaptation related to extreme heat.
  - She will also attend Working group focus sessions, attend internal Hyphae meetings and give feedback, review input to outreach plan, as well as reviewing and commenting on our draft plan

With support from Hyphae, Hakimipour will review and synthesize existing federal, state, and local plans, policies, and guidelines related to climate adaptation and extreme heat to identify synergies between ongoing agency projects and our efforts. This process will <comment_start id=kix.fovrwbt1qxro><comment_start id=kix.hiqq9a7arlwr>maximize efficiency by seeing areas where Hyphae work could overlap w/ ongoing agency projects.<comment_end id=kix.fovrwbt1qxro> UDD will lead this effort, leveraging her existing contacts on the Working Group and institutional knowledge of the Fresno space.    <comment_end id=kix.hiqq9a7arlwr>

Early data gathering will ensure that foundational layers, such as existing environmental and transportation data, can be analyzed quickly and effectively. The review will include key frameworks and reports, such as:

By synthesizing these documents, Hyphae will identify areas where ongoing agency efforts align with the project’s goals, maximizing efficiency and reducing redundancy. This integration ensures that regulatory priorities are met while tailoring actionable strategies to Fresno County’s unique needs.
Categories of reports and data to be reviewed include:

3.  **Jurisdictional and Political Data**: Land use plans, zoning regulations, and inter-agency frameworks.
4.  **Demographic and Socioeconomic Data**: Census data, population dynamics, and social vulnerability assessments.
5.  **Transportation Data**: Corridors, infrastructure projects, and active transportation plans.
6.  **Community Networks**: Existing partnerships with NGOs, health centers, and faith-based organizations.<comment_start id=kix.x0hilng6ug7y>

Designed as both a practical tool for local decision-makers and a resource for community advocates, this deliverable bridges technical analysis and community engagement. By equipping stakeholders with accessible knowledge, this review empowers Fresno County to advance equitable and impactful climate adaptation strategies.<comment_end id=kix.x0hilng6ug7y> 
## Task 3: Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis
### **Approach**
The **Heat Vulnerability Assessment** employs a highly detailed and community-focused approach to understand and address the complexities of extreme heat exposure. Traditional heat mapping tools often lack the resolution necessary for actionable, community-level interventions, limiting their utility in rural and mixed-use environments such as Fresno County. To overcome these limitations, our analysis integrates advanced data sources, modeling techniques, and iterative community engagement to deliver unprecedented precision in identifying heat-vulnerable areas and populations. By synthesizing meteorological data and land use classifications with innovative population disaggregation methods and community insights, the assessment provides a detailed understanding of how heat is distributed and experienced across diverse local contexts.
Our core strategy is to to bring together data from multiple sources and resolutions to create ultra-high resolution insights. We also leverage this process for both environmental and social variables. The workflow development, software automation and hardware server infrastructure for this work is funded by our NSF SBIR and other grants. 
This advanced methodology moves beyond surface and air heat mapping by incorporating thermal comfort variables such as wind flow, humidity, and radiant temperature into neighborhood-specific simulations. These models are further enhanced through continuous engagement with community stakeholders, ensuring the integration of hyperlocal knowledge about demographic patterns and daily behaviors. The analysis also employs state-of-the-art tools such as digital twins for simulations that model the effects of sun, shade, and heat reradiation, creating a three-dimensional understanding of the built environment. By integrating technical rigor with community relevance, our dual emphasis ensures the delivery of practical, targeted solutions tailored to Fresno County’s unique challenges.
See our supplement package for detailed maps of this process
#### Methodology for Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis
##### **Resolution and Scope**
Because lower spatial resolution analysis requires less time and computational resources than high resolution per unit of area analyzed, our approach is to conduct lower resolution analyses over larger areas, and then to use those analyses to target smaller areas, where higher resolution analyses are performed at an economical scale. For this purpose we will describe three “levels” of analysis: a) low resolution, which can be done at the whole county scale and can cover many years of historical or future projections, b) high resolution analyses, which can cover just the 5 target project areas, covering each hour of a few days at different times of year, and c) medium resolution data, which will fall somewhere between high and low resolution in its scope. 
##### **Low resolution thermal comfort data**
Broadly available high temporal resolution but low spatial resolution data from earth and climate modeling data is in the 1-30km resolution. This data is useful because it is specific to health heat risk and thermal comfort variables but is too broad for targeted action. Examples include ERA5-Heat from Europe, which is global scale and NWS Heatrisk which is CONUS scale.
Although low resolution spatially, this data is modeled daily or weekly and across a wide temporal scale, ERA5 for example goes back 40± years and also has data for future scenarios based on climate change models. This can provide a reliable mosaic of large swaths of area that have and will regularly have heat stress into the future. 
While we consider this data low resolution it is actually far more advanced, detailed, relevant and helpful than current county and census derived maps like CHAT which are currently used by most counties, for things like heat warning, because they are topographically , meteorologically and environmentally driven, rather than delineated by political boundaries. 
They aren't however high enough resolution for more intervention based work which needs to happen at the town, neighborhood, block and property level scale.
We are an innovation-driven firm and will continue to incorporate new metrics and models and resolutions of data. New metrics Thermal Stress Duration (TSD) was just published in October 2024 and expands on UTCI to look at how long over the days and days people are exposed and have greater risk.  
##### **Medium resolution heat data**
We then gain further insights with medium resolution (1km down to 30m) satellite derived heat data like ecostress, landsat, which are heat specific, but only include surface or air temperature, not thermal comfort and therefore don't tell the true health heat risk. These maps can be used to identify more specific hotspots at the neighborhood and block scale that will also likely have heat stress. 
This data should be used with some caution because of the spatial resolution and surface characteristic quality of the data. For example, building roofs might make it hot, but people aren't normally on the roof, they are next to more shaded and cooler building walls or under trees, where a surface measurement of the top of the crown, might be quite hot, but that's because it's blocking the sun from the cooler and understory which is not being sensed by the satellite. 
But if these limitations are known, considered and not ultimately used for final intervention scale analysis, this dataset is really useful because it is relatively high temporal resolution, often gathered weekly or monthly and because it can be used to estimate unmeasured variables like air temperature which can then be fed into our higher resolution models discussed later. 
##### **Other medium resolution data **
Local climate zones (LCZ) data is calculated at 30  meter resolution for a single time period and reflects 17 categories of local topology concerning feature types (buildings and vegetation) heights (tall, medium, short, none) and  spacing (dense, medium, sparse). This can be used to stratify heat data (such as surface temperature) to prioritize where interventions are most likely to be plausible and effective. Similarly we use 100 meter dasymetric population density data and Cropscape crop mapping to estimate where people live or are likely to be working outdoors to adjust risk factors for heat stress estimates at the 30 meter scale.
##### **Hyper-local high resolution environmental modeling.**
After choosing neighborhoods or corridors to target interventions, discussed in task description, we come back to further modeling work where we then utilize our own novel high resolution modeling to develop meter scale simulations. This includes shade and UTCI models at 1-10 meter resolution. 
This work combines the development of 3d digital twins of trees, buildings, terrain and other features from lidar data and simulation modeling of UTCI variables including air temperature, humidity and radiant temperature. These are novel datasets that we generate from our software pipeline.      
To conduct a simulation of 1-meter scale mean radiant temperature in the 5 target project areas, we will take aerial LIDAR data from the USGS 3D Elevation program gathered from 2019-2023, and use it to generate 3D models of the target areas,  including building heights, and canopy leaf area index. We will then find selected historical days in which there was minimal cloud cover to simulate, which correspond to time periods where extreme heat was recorded. We will obtain hourly meteorological data from the nearest weather service stations which include regional wind speed, air temperature, pressure and humidity. We will also obtain estimates of total incoming shortwave radiation from the [CBE clima tool](https://clima.cbe.berkeley.edu/). These meteorological data points will be inputted along with the 3D model into the Urban Multi-scale Environmental Predictor (UMEP)’s SOlar and LongWave Environmental Irradiance Geometry model (SOLWEIG) solver. The outputs of this will be 24 hourly estimates of mean radiant temperature in human occupied elevations on a 1 meter grid for each of the 5 target project areas. These can be combined with estimates of wind speed, humidity and air temperature to produce 1 meter UTCI maps. From these 1 meter grids we can then calculate cumulative heat stress for specific pedestrian routes, discussed more in the pedestrian section. 
Here we can differentiate the temperature in the middle of the street from those under a tree on the sidewalk. We can see how hot it is on a basketball court vs under a grove of trees. We can also see how the thermal comfort is different at different times and how it changes how temperatures accumulate and radiate over the course of the day.
With these models we target specific low tree areas of a neighborhood or segments of a corridor for intervention. We also work with communities to see where they live, work and play. Discussed further in the pedestrian section.
In addition to running these models for prioritization and opportunity studies of the existing conditions, we will also run prospective intervention design prototypes selected from the 5 project focus areas to run through the simulations to estimate the benefits of designed interventions. 
#### Social and infrastructural vulnerability 
Before doing further environmental mapping and modeling, it is generally better to use this data in combination with social vulnerability data to further prioritize neighborhood and town sized hotspot areas to target for interventions because heat vulnerability is both a physical and social variable. 
##### **Census Data**
We then conduct social and infrastructure vulnerability assessments. This is normally unfortunately done with census data because that's all that exists to identify things like age, occupation, race and income level of the population. Census, census tract and census block group data can be helpful in larger dense cities, but is extremely challenging in low population dense rural areas, providing a disservice to some of the most impacted rural farming communities. For example the City of Fresno may have 40 census tracks then a giant rural area is one, which doesn't allow us to spatially target a population. We will use census data to begin, but we then also disaggregate the census data to provide a more nuanced estimation of these aspects of heat-risk needs. 
##### **Census Disaggregation**
We utilize new methods developed by health and emergency response researchers to gain further granularity into the social fabric of a community.  One method (Popgrid) is from Harvard researchers who utilized census data in combination with building footprint and land use data to "estimate" the location of people to 100 meter resolution. The method basically says there's x thousand people in this census tract, there's high rise buildings in this location, and there's single family homes, they probably have about this amount of people in them and distribute the people into the buildings accordingly. This method doesn't know who lives where or of what race or age, but it's really helpful for rural areas where there's giant swaths of land that make up census tracts but generally most people in a small town or scattered farm houses. This method can use census data to target vulnerable populations broadly, then building location data to target interventions. There is another method that also statistically distributes populations called RTI synth-pop that retains census level data variables, but has limitations in its spatial distribution. We will incorporate both datasets and compare the results to support decision making.
##### **Sensitive Receptor & Worker Exposure Locations**
We will also utilize other data sets of specific locations of particularly sensitive receptors. We have developed a national database of Senior centers, schools, parks and day cares. We also have a database of warehouses and farms. These datasets will be used to further prioritize communities for heat risk needs at higher resolution than available in census
Increased exposure
People who work outdoors, are experiencing homelessness, or are emergency responders are at higher risk. 
Sensitive receptors
People who are under 5, over 65, pregnant, or have chronic health conditions are more vulnerable. This work is normally limited by census tract level and far worse in dispersed rural areas but synthetic population data can help us be more specific 
Socioeconomic burden
People who are low income, live in low resource communities, or have disabilities are more vulnerable.
Lack of access to cooling
People who don't have access to or can't afford air conditioning are more susceptible. 
Historic underinvestment
Communities of color and low-income communities have less greenspace and tree planting, which contributes to hotter urban temperatures. 
Public Health Data
Through ongoing collaboration with UC Merced we also have access to some healthcare related heat exposure data.
See our supplement package for detailed maps of this process
##### **5 Project Area Modeling outcomes**
The 1m mean radiant temperature data combined with the digital twin allows the following insights at the project scale:
1.  Hotspots, hot routes, cool spots, and cool routes will all become identifiable and can be used for planning pedestrian and bike routes and gathering spaces. 
2.  Built environment effects on heat stress (such as shade from different species and ages of trees, buildings and their relative positions can be evaluated and used to inform the design of the tree planting interventions.
3.  At the project scale, iterations of tree planting designs can be produced in-silico, and run through the simulation again. This gives us pre and post assessment of planting plan impacts on heat stress and allows the design process to be optimized for maximal benefits. 
This approach avoids overwhelming "big red spot" data, offering localized, actionable insights tailored to community priorities.
##### **Countywide heat vulnerability maps and segmentation**
We combine the neighborhood scale environmental data and social data to create a countywide heat vulnerability map that is down to 100 meter resolution and driven by environmental metrics and social metrics and built environment metrics that is far more useful than politically driven boundaries. 
This can be used for many different uses from health research to targeted outreach campaigns to cooling center location development to other infrastructure improvement planning goals. We can group the data by ROW segments for transportation corridor targeted work or by parcel for property level targeted work. 
### **Deliverables**
Baseline deliverables for this task will include a list of areas most vulnerable to extreme heat for Fresno COG and working group review. We will also leverage our existing web platform for interaction and accessibility.
  - **Methodology for Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis (Q3, draft in Q2)**
  - **Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis Memo **including methodology and results;** list of vulnerable areas**, summarizing findings in the  and identifying priority areas for intervention.
##### **Extreme Heat Vulnerability Analysis Summary Memo**
We will compile maps and methodology and package into digestible distributable docs. We can also serve the data from our platform AdaptOS for the duration of the project and then submit it as a report to the county at the end of the project. 
##### **Enhanced Outreach Platforms**
The data and visualizations will be uploaded to the project website and other online forums, forming the foundation for the public outreach plan. This plan will outline the following activities:
  - **Project Website:** Interactive tools for community feedback and updates, including notifications for extreme heat events and interactive shade maps.
  - **Social Media:** Targeted campaigns on platforms such as Facebook, Instagram, and Twitter (X) to disseminate updates and engage residents in discussions.
  - **Workshops:** Virtual and in-person sessions conducted in English and Spanish, with additional languages provided as needed, to ensure inclusivity.
  - **PopUp Events:** Outreach at existing community gatherings to maximize visibility and accessibility.
  - **Online Interactive Tools:** Engaging platforms for residents to explore modeled data, provide input, and identify community priorities.
The outreach plan above specifies targeted strategies for engaging hard-to-reach disadvantaged communities, including leveraging culturally relevant media outlets such as Radio Bilingüe, Fresnoland, and ABC30. It will also utilize partnerships with CBOs to guide linguistically appropriate methods and outreach in priority neighborhoods. Feedback from the Working Group will be incorporated to finalize and refine the outreach plan before its implementation.
## <comment_start id=kix.duhbwn4q74yg>Task 4: Conduct Pedestrian and Bicyclist Traffic Analysis and Location Analysis<comment_end id=kix.duhbwn4q74yg>
### **Approach to Pedestrian and Bike Analysis**
#### Methodology Development<comment_start id=kix.65pk2cvwvw2a>
We will further develop a methodology after Task 2, report review and data collection occurs. Below is a preliminary approach for conducting pedestrian and bicyclist traffic analysis and location analysis, while ensuring alignment with extreme heat vulnerability metrics and Fresno COG guidelines. During the method development phase we will refine the methodology in collaboration with COG<comment_end id=kix.65pk2cvwvw2a> and the working group. 
#### Data for Analysis
We will source data from all available resources, some of which are in development but hopefully available by the time of the plan, some options included below.
We will leverage the data from ongoing and existing Active Transportation Plans in the City, County and State including but not limited to:
  - Caltrans District 6 Active Transportation Plan
  - Central Valley Bikeways Project  (part of high speed rail program)
  - City of Fresno Active Transportation Plan
  - County of Fresno COG Final Fresno County Regional Active Transportation Plans:
    1.  City of Coalinga, City of Firebaugh, City of Fowler, City of Huron, City of Kerman, City of Kingsburg, City of Mendota, City of Orange Cove, City of Parlier, City of San Joaquin, City of Sanger.
    2.  Unincorporated Counties: most of which also align with priority heat risk communities identified by Trees Fresno.
We will also leverage other state level resources including: 
  - The California Active Transportation Data Portal (CATDP)
    1.  California statewide active transportation count database,
    2.  State Highway Pedestrian Exposure Estimates (SafeTREC).
We will also leverage other novel data sets where possible. We would like to use these but can not commit to doing all of these budgetarily, but may include them in some project locations pending method development and working group consultation:
  - OpenStreeMaps (OSM): OSM has some but not all walking paths, and cycleways in Fresno county mapped 
  - Streetlight data: While the budget doesn't allow for streetlight type data to be bought for the entire county, we have experience using it for sidewalk and cycle path prioritizations, and may be able to purchase small amounts for some of the 5 project areas. 
  - Tile2net: For the 5 target areas we may be able to use tile2net deep learning algorithm to create network maps of pedestrian paths and crosswalks from aerial imagery. 
\[image\]
### **Approach to <comment_start id=kix.65pk2cvwvw2a>Location Analysis**
We will combine the above <comment_end id=kix.65pk2cvwvw2a>pedestrian and bicyclist traffic analyses with the extreme heat vulnerability analysis to identify preliminary communities with overlapping vulnerable and active transportation corridor demands. We will also identify high-risk heat communities who may not have plans, as they may be even more disadvantaged. 
We first define hotspots through geospatial analysis of heat and pedestrian analysis to provide a quantitative list of 25 to 30 neighborhoods or corridors distributed between different county regions. We will then further prioritization into five target neighborhoods or corridors for project development through opportunity analysis and community input.
#### Hotspot Prioritization
We can then run areas fairly easily by political metrics. Most vulnerable cities, census tracts, supervisorial districts, whatever the working group decides but it's driven by actual environment metrics not those political ones.
  - Begin with a pool of 20–30 neighborhoods or corridors distributed across Fresno County, identified through environmental metrics derived from Tasks 3 and 4 (e.g., extreme heat exposure, air quality, lack of tree canopy).
  - Apply political metrics, such as census tracts, supervisorial districts, and CalEnviroScreen scores, as secondary criteria, guided by the Working Group.
#### Opportunity Analysis
This refinement process can be further refined by an opportunity analysis that is both practical and complicated community outreach driven.** **Opportunity analysis can then be done based on a number of variables. From our experience on the L.A. county Depaving project and others, these are some of the criteria that help refine project locations:
  - Where is there other planned infrastructure improvement work? 
  - Where are there particularly activated, involved and or concerned communities? 
  - Where are other nonprofits or agencies trying to work? 
  - Where could our tree shading work help drive another project already underway or in the pipeline
  - Where does a school district or town already have interest or money coming in for similar work? (to leverage existing resources)
  - Where does a school district or town *not* already have interest or money coming in for similar work? (to mitigate lack of existing resources)
#### Community and Stakeholder Engagement
  - CBO will engage community members and stakeholders through workshops, public meetings, in-person outreach to refine and validate project selections.
  - This integration of community insights reduces redundant data collection, focusing resources where they are most needed and ensuring alignment with local priorities.
  - Incorporate public input to ensure proposed projects reflect local priorities and address the unique challenges of disadvantaged communities.
#### People Counters 
  - There is not enough funding in this RFP to deploy these devices but we would like to coordinate with COG to deploy Pedestrian Counters through this or a partnership program (we have experience working with TXDOT and TAMU researchers with EcoCounters). This will involve providing COG with information from the heat vulnerability and pedestrian analyses that identifies priority locations for gathering people-counter data. 
  - We are doing a before and after analysis of tree planting corridor in Dallas that we would like to try replicate with the COG
### **Deliverables**
A single document summarizing methodology, analysis results, and identified vulnerable corridors, tailored for use by Fresno COG and project stakeholders.
  - Pedestrian and Bicyclist Traffic Analysis and Location Analysis Summary Memo
  - Refine the list to five target neighborhoods or corridors for focused project development.
  - Also include the longer list of 30 priority areas for other funding initiatives.
## <comment_start id=kix.wkh8pxmv77ox>Task 5: Develop a Draft List of Tree Canopy and Vegetative Cover Projects <comment_end id=kix.wkh8pxmv77ox>
### **Approach**
This task continues the refinement of more specific project locations and corridors in the most heat vulnerable communities identified in Task 3 that also overlap with Pedestrian Corridor needs in Task 4.  This task will then evaluate where there is low tree canopy and vegetative cover in those hotspots. The process leverages a data driven approach while also incorporating public input and leveraging local knowledge to ensure efficiency and alignment with community priorities.
Mona from Tree Fresno will utilize their existing network of community and institutional contacts across the Central Valley to streamline the analysis process. Her local knowledge will complement technical approaches by identifying areas with strong community interest and readiness for collaboration. There are already a recurring list of communities that our Trees Fresno and EJ partners regularly work with that are hot, under-treed and regularly on various screening tools lists in Fresno County including Armona, Biola, Bowles, Burrel, Camden, Cantua Creek, Conejo, Easton, Helm, Kettleman City, Lanare, Laton, Raisin City, Riverdale, Rolinda, Three Rocks, Tranquility, and West Park. This is a sample list to show our understanding of the community, we will work with the COG and utilize a data-driven approach to heat to develop a final list. 
That data driven approach will leverage evaluating greenness metrics along corridors in the target areas. We will utilize high resolution canopy cover maps from Calpoly and USFS.  We will also run Green Streetview Index in the 5 project areas already prioritized to assist in design and evaluation of planting projects.  We will also make Hyphae’s proprietary tree species database and cutting edge tree growth algorithms available to assist the local experts with developing tree lists that are native, drought-tolerant, site suitable and able to provide maximal cost-benefit ratios for each project area.
### **Deliverables**
**Draft List of Tree Canopy and Vegetative Cover Projects**, completed during This document will include a detailed narrative, prioritization framework, and visual aids, designed to guide future implementation efforts and ensure alignment with Fresno County’s climate adaptation goals.
  - **Project profiles** (fact sheets) will include a mix of rural and urban areas and provide high-level costing for implementation.
  - The **Draft List of Tree Canopy and Vegetative Cover Projects** will include:
      - High level conceptual design of planting locations and strategies, emphasizing native and drought-tolerant species.
      - Maps and visuals illustrating project locations and expected benefits.
      - Integration opportunities with existing programs and planned infrastructure projects.
      - Maintenance recommendations to ensure long-term sustainability.
## <comment_start id=kix.3sgzpdut9tvq>Task 6: Develop Planning Level Costs for the Identified Shade Improvement Projects<comment_end id=kix.3sgzpdut9tvq>
### **Approach**
This task focuses on developing high-level planning cost estimates for the proposed tree canopy and vegetative cover projects, providing a clear framework for future implementation and funding efforts.
**  
**Hyphae Design Lab will draw on its experience managing costing for project typologies, including work on DepaveLA and West Oakland, to deliver practical and scalable cost estimates.
  - Retain contractors and engineers to assist in pricing high-impact projects, prioritizing local businesses where possible.
  - Focus on conceptual-level costing, including costs for tree planting, vegetative cover installation, and maintenance, tailored to site-specific conditions.
#### Conceptual Cost Estimates:
Costs for these strategies and elements will be researched by several methods and then compiled and compared. Comprehensive costs estimates will be received from a number of small contractors. We may also contact The County Public Works Department, Caltrans, and other agencies to obtain typical costs from similar historical and relevant projects. Spreadsheets and other data received from these contractors and departments will be analyzed and refined based upon projects of relevant scale.  Some commercial contractors costing databases and software programs will also be queried to obtain typical costs for project elements. Additionally, several published studies with actual installed cost data produced by green infrastructure nonprofits will be consulted. These data will be combined in a comprehensive cost analysis spreadsheet. 
#### Contractor Outreach & Collaboration:
Qualified contractors will be vetted according to experience working on projects of different scales, including residential, commercial and municipal. Preference will be given to small local contractors and contractors with CBE/DBE/MBE/WBE status. It is preferred that these projects will benefit local contractors, as they may have more community ties and that the community would have more ownership of the project. It is also preferred that by having disadvantaged contractors on the project, that there will also be more support amongst the diverse populations which live in these communities.  It is also recognized that perhaps these small contractors may not have the experience with larger projects, so it is possible that they could be subcontractors to larger firms if that proves to be more effective.
### **Deliverables**
  - **Planning Level Costs for Identified Shade Improvement Projects**:  
    A summary of high-level cost estimates, designed to guide project prioritization and resource allocation. The format will be short reports with diagrams.
This collection of high level solutions is an approach that presents actionable funding information to support high impact projects toward Fresno County’s climate adaptation goals while respecting budget constraints.
## <comment_start id=kix.95w3i02j2q68>Task 7: Select Five Projects in the Most Vulnerable Areas and Develop Project Profiles<comment_end id=kix.95w3i02j2q68>
### **Approach**
In collaboration with the Working Group, we will identify five projects in Fresno County’s most heat-vulnerable areas, ensuring the process is both efficient and community-driven. By leveraging the extensive local relationships and knowledge of our partners, such as Tree Fresno and BOCC, we will prioritize communities ready to engage and align interventions with community needs, reducing the time and costs typically associated with redundant analysis.
Our approach will integrate data-driven insights with community input, utilizing proven methodologies and existing resources, such as those developed in STEP and Dallas, to inform project selection and design. Recognizing the budget constraints, we will focus on delivering high-level project profiles and conceptual plans rather than schematic designs. These profiles will include a neighborhood overview, extreme heat vulnerability analysis, conceptual intervention descriptions, and preliminary cost estimates.
This approach balances feasibility with ambition, ensuring that the deliverables reflect actionable, community-informed strategies. Furthermore, the project profiles will serve as a foundation for future funding opportunities, demonstrating the scalability and potential impact of the proposed interventions. Hyphae’s extensive experience with similar projects ensures that these efforts align with best practices and position Fresno County for long-term success in addressing extreme heat challenges.
The process for selecting and developing profiles for five pilot projects will combine data-driven analysis, community input, and the expertise of the Working Group and partners:
#### Project Identification
  - **Collaboration with the Working Group:  
    **Over the course of quarterly meetings, the Working Group will evaluate extreme heat vulnerability data, prioritize community needs, and discuss feasible interventions.
  - **Leveraging Local Expertise:  
    **Partners like Tree Fresno and BOCC will provide critical local insights, identifying communities that are both highly vulnerable and ready to engage. Mona Cummings’ connections with farmworker communities and BOCC’s extensive network across the Central Valley will streamline the identification of potential target neighborhoods, blending analytical findings with real-world readiness.
  - **Balancing Rural and Urban Projects:  
    **Projects will include a mix of rural and urban settings, ensuring an equitable distribution of interventions that reflect Fresno County’s diverse needs.
#### Criteria for Selection
A transparent and structured framework will guide project selection, incorporating:
  - Heat vulnerability scores based on physical and socioeconomic metrics.
  - Community priorities as identified during public engagement activities.
  - Feasibility of interventions within the available budget.
  - Scalability and potential for long-term impact.
#### Developing Project Profiles
Each project profile will include the following elements:
  - **Neighborhood Overview:** A snapshot of the community, its demographics, and its unique vulnerabilities to extreme heat.
  - **Vulnerability Analysis:** Heat vulnerability scores and key factors contributing to those vulnerabilities.
  - **Proposed Interventions:** Conceptual designs for interventions such as tree planting, shade structures, cooling centers, or other greening strategies tailored to local needs.
  - **Preliminary Cost Estimates:** High-level costing aligned with budget constraints, providing an accessible understanding of resource needs.
  - **Scalability and Next Steps:** Strategies for leveraging these profiles into future funding opportunities or expanded project scopes.
#### Community Engagement in Project Development
  - Community feedback gathered during BOCC-led outreach activities will directly inform the design and prioritization of interventions.
  - Input from Working Group discussions will ensure that the selected projects are aligned with Fresno County’s goals for equity, sustainability, and resilience.
### **Deliverables**
Profiles will be compiled into both technical and public-facing formats, while fact sheets will summarize each project in an accessible way, highlighting their goals, expected impacts, and benefits for potential stakeholders and funders.
  - **Criteria for Project Selection (Q3):** A framework guiding the identification of projects based on vulnerability analysis and community input.
  - **Project Profiles for Five Projects (Q5, draft in Q4):** Detailed conceptual plans with high-level costing and descriptions of proposed interventions.
  - **Project Fact Sheets for Five Projects (Q4):** Public-facing summaries highlighting the purpose, benefits, and cost-effectiveness of each project.
## Task 8: Develop the Draft Plan and Publish the Draft for Public Review
### **Approach**
This task synthesizes all prior work into a comprehensive draft plan that outlines recommendations for short, medium, and long-term strategies to implement and maintain proposed shade improvement projects. The plan will highlight co-benefits across public health, ecosystems, social equity, economic resilience, and greenhouse gas reduction. Following the plan’s completion, it will be published for a 30-day public review period to gather meaningful feedback from stakeholders and community members.
#### Draft Plan Development
Hyphae will compile findings from earlier tasks to create a structured, actionable draft plan, including:
  - Recommendations for implementation timelines (short, medium, and long-term).
  - Maintenance strategies for proposed projects.
  - Integration of active transportation and public transit improvements.
  - Co-benefits analysis across public health, ecosystems, equity, economy, and climate.
We will meet in focus sessions with Fresno COG and the Working Group to review and refine recommendations, ensuring alignment with local goals and constraints.
#### Public Review Preparation
  - Prepare the draft plan for a 30-day public review period, ensuring accessibility and clarity for all stakeholders.
  - Develop supporting materials, including an executive summary and a user-friendly online feedback form.
  - Collaborate with community-based organizations (CBOs) to distribute the draft plan and maximize participation in the review process.
**Public Comment Collection and Documentation**
  - Collect and organize feedback from the public review process, including comments from online submissions, open houses, and stakeholder meetings.
  - Summarize public input and incorporate key recommendations into the final plan, ensuring transparency in how feedback is addressed.
### **Deliverables**
  - **Draft Plan (Q7, annotated comments for final):** A detailed, actionable document of short, medium, and long term strategies including implementation recommendations, co-benefits analysis, and maintenance strategies
  - **Public Comments Documentation:** A compiled summary of public feedback, including responses and recommendations for final revisions.
## Task 9: Presentation and Policy Board Acceptance
Hyphae and partners will present the draft final plan to Fresno COG’s Transportation Technical Committee, Policy Advisory Committee, and Policy Board for review and acceptance, as well as incorporating feedback into the final plan.
### **Approach**
#### Draft Final Plan Presentation
Deliver a PowerPoint presentation summarizing key components of the draft plan, including:
  - Project goals and findings.
  - Implementation strategies and co-benefits analysis.
#### Feedback Integration
  - Collect feedback from the committee presentations.
  - Revise the draft final plan to align with stakeholder input and regional priorities.
### **Deliverables**
1.  **PowerPoint Presentation:** Summarizing key aspects of the draft plan.
2.  **Final Plan:** Revised and finalized based on committee feedback.
This task ensures the draft plan is thoroughly reviewed and accepted by decision-making bodies, setting the stage for effective implementation.
# <comment_start id=kix.yw9inmt7bdhe>E. Management Approach<comment_end id=kix.yw9inmt7bdhe>
### **Overview of Management Approach**
Brent Bucknum and Ivan Heitmann will provide project oversight. They will help develop the initial work plan, budget, schedule and then do quarterly meetings to adjust timeline and work plan and strategy as needed after working group meetings. Eric Dorfman, CFO, will be responsible for producing quarry invoices and financial reports. Merissa will work with Sheila to coordinate outward facing elements of the project with COG, CBO’s and partners, with strategic support and oversight from Brent. Ivan will oversee the technical geospatial deliverables with oversight and support from DF our Chief Scientist. Merissa and Ivan, with support from with trees fresno will develop the greening strategies for the communities
### **Organization of Management**
  - **Ivan Heitmann (Project Manager and Technical Design Lead, 325 hours):** An experienced project manager specializing in community supported environmental design, Ivan will oversee all project activities. Ivan will serve as the primary liaison with Fresno COG, ensuring adherence to deadlines, quality control, and consistent communication. Beyond coordination and project management, Ivan will also conduct environmental simulations and technical analyses for urban design solutions. Ivan will be actively involved in **all stages of the project**, contributing his expertise to tasks ranging from extreme heat vulnerability assessments and transportation analyses to the development of tree canopy projects and costing frameworks. His consistent involvement ensures technical precision and seamless integration of analyses across deliverables.
  - **Mei Visco (Project Management Specialist and Ecosystem Designer, 24 hours)**: An experienced project manager specializing in environmental justice and climate adaptation, Mei will supervise project management and team coordination. Mei will also assist with collaboration and community engagement, conducting pedestrian and bicyclist traffic analysis and location analysis, and developing a draft list of tree canopy and vegetative cover projects. Mei’s proven ability to manage interdisciplinary teams and engage diverse stakeholders will ensure that all deliverables align with Fresno COG’s objectives and reflect community priorities.
  - **Brent Bucknum (Principal, 140 hours):** Provides strategic oversight and ensures alignment with project goals and stakeholder priorities.
  - **Daniel Fleischer (Chief Science Officer, 263 hours):** Leads environmental assessment methodologies, heat vulnerability modeling, and technical integration.
  - **Merissa MacDonald (Hyphae Arborist, 101 hours):** Develops urban forestry strategies and oversees vegetation planning. In addition to her technical expertise, Merissa will lead **extensive community engagement** efforts, ensuring local input informs project outcomes. She will also participate in **drafting the plan** and contribute to the **Presentation and Policy Board Acceptance** phase, integrating urban forestry insights into project recommendations and public presentations.
  - **Juan Penaloza (Modeling Specialist, 44 hours):** Provides computational modeling expertise, focusing on heat vulnerability assessments. 
  - **Eric Olson (Civil Engineer, 70 hours):** Supports infrastructure-related costing and project feasibility assessments.
  - **Local Subconsultants and DBE Firms:** <comment_start id=kix.aurrfcshglxr>We have budgeted over $50,000 for subcontractors<comment_end id=kix.aurrfcshglxr> to assist in community engagement, translation services, mobility analysis, and Tree Fresno’s local arborist expertise, contributing to equitable project outcomes.
### **Work Assignments and Staffing**
#### **Team Assignments by Task:**
  - **Task 0 (Coordination):** <comment_start id=kix.smc2tku6gki0>Ivan Heitmann <comment_end id=kix.smc2tku6gki0>leads bi-monthly stakeholder meetings and internal team coordination, ensuring seamless communication and efficient management of project activities.
  - **Task 1 (Community Engagement):** Merissa MacDonald and local partners lead engagement efforts, conducting workshops and public outreach to gather input and foster community involvement.

### **Partnership Structure**
For more detail on partner experience, see Summary of Qualifications section
#### Urban Diversity Design
Sheila Hakimipour
[urbandiversitydesign.com](http://urbandiversitydesign.com)
She describes herself as "boots on the ground" for engineers and design firms, specializing in connecting engineers, city officials, and community groups. Her expertise includes developing engagement plans and collaborating with community-based organizations (CBOs) and agencies on projects like Complete Streets and Corridor Revitalization. Currently, she is working on multiple projects with the Fresno Council of Governments (COG), including a hazard risk study with ARUP that examines flooding, fire, and, to some extent, heat. Through this work, she has identified the limitations of existing heat vulnerability maps, highlighting the need for more effective tools to address these challenges. Additionally, she is about to embark on a Vision Zero plan for the City of Fresno.
Like Hyphae and Binational, she prioritizes engaging the community through events rather than relying solely on project-specific meetings, which often attract only a small group of passionate participants and fail to gather input from a diverse and representative population. She does not claim to represent the community but instead focuses on supporting and organizing the efforts of CBOs while attending pop-up events to represent the project and ensure its visibility.
*Role on project*
Sheila’s experience coordinating agencies and CBOs will be helpful in supporting the working group formation and operation in support of the COG and hyphae.
Her experience with other COG and City projects will help identify synergies, ensuring the heat plan complements and enhances previous and ongoing initiatives. She will act as a key resource in developing the engagement plan and coordinating with CBOs. 
#### Binational of California
Ricardo Castorena (President/CEO) and Rico Peralta (Senior Director of Impact & Partnerships)
<https://binationalca.org/binational-team/>
**Experience  
**Under the leadership of Rico Peralta and Ricardo Castorena, BOCC has over a decade of experience delivering culturally informed outreach and engagement across California’s Central Valley, focusing on eight counties including Fresno, Kern, and Tulare. BOCC specializes in reaching vulnerable populations, including Latinos, Indigenous farmworkers, and non-English-speaking households, with targeted programs that address disaster preparedness, public health, and equity.
**<comment_start id=kix.7q00q1oel5lo>Role on Project  
**BOCC will play a central role in ensuring the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan is community-driven and informed by diverse voices:
  - **Community Liaison:** BOCC will act as the primary interface between the project team and local residents, ensuring authentic engagement through their established networks and event calendar.
  - **Outreach Integration:** By embedding project outreach into existing events like resource fairs, tax preparation sessions, and cultural celebrations, BOCC will facilitate data collection on heat impacts and gather hyperlocal feedback.
  - **Translation and Accessibility:** BOCC will provide on-site translation and interpretation services in Spanish, Hmong, Punjabi, and most prevalent Indigenous languages, ensuring outreach efforts are inclusive.
  - **Working Group Support:** BOCC will help form and operate the project’s working group, bringing in key community stakeholders and providing logistical and cultural insights to guide decision-making.
  - **Facilitation and Reporting:** BOCC will host and facilitate community meetings, deliver critical feedback to the technical team, and document all outreach activities through surveys, testimonials, and visual evidence.<comment_end id=kix.7q00q1oel5lo>
**Key Highlights of Expertise:**
  - **Community Engagement Expertise:** BOCC has engaged over 300,000 residents, prioritizing populations with higher rates of poverty, disabilities, and linguistic isolation. Their culturally resonant approach ensures meaningful outreach and trust-building within marginalized communities.
  - **Disaster Preparedness Leadership:** As a trusted partner in the LISTOS California campaign, BOCC developed multilingual disaster readiness messaging in seven Indigenous languages, Spanish, Hmong, and Punjabi, ensuring accessibility for disinvested communities and preparing families for emergencies.
  - **Health and Equity Campaigns:** BOCC has led public health initiatives like the “Get Out The Vaccine” campaign, facilitating the administration of 72,000 vaccines, distributing 60,000 COVID-19 test kits, and hosting 75 large-scale vaccination events.
  - **Event-Based Engagement:** Embedded outreach efforts into culturally resonant events such as Toys for Tots (4,000 attendees annually), health fairs, and free tax preparation sessions, consistently generating participation levels far exceeding conventional outreach methods.
  - **Data and Reporting Excellence:** BOCC leverages advanced systems like OPX and Pear Suite to manage complex multi-agency grants (e.g., USDA, EDD), track engagement, and deliver actionable data insights alongside high-quality financial and narrative reports.
BOCC’s trusted presence and expertise in culturally competent outreach ensures scalable, effective engagement strategies tailored to the needs of California’s most vulnerable communities. Their demonstrated leadership in disaster preparedness, health equity, and event-based engagement makes them an unparalleled partner in addressing critical challenges across the Central Valley.
#### Tree Fresno
Mona Cummings, CEO
<https://treefresno.org/>
**Experience**
Tree Fresno, under the leadership of Mona Cummings, has over 30 years of experience advancing urban forestry, environmental education, and community stewardship initiatives across Fresno County and the broader Central Valley. With a focus on improving quality of life and environmental equity, Tree Fresno has established itself as a regional leader in climate-resilient urban greening. Their notable achievements and partnerships include:
Urban Forestry Expertise:
  - Partnered with city governments, public agencies, and community groups to implement large-scale urban greening projects, including the planting of thousands of trees in underserved neighborhoods to mitigate heat island effects and improve air quality.
  - Awarded $1 million by the Valley Air District in Spring 2023 to implement the Valley Air Urban Greening Project throughout South Central Fresno, as part of California Climate Investments. This initiative involves planting 1,200 drought-tolerant trees for residents within the AB 617 boundary and 500 community trees, addressing greenhouse gas emissions and public health disparities.
  - Delivered vegetation management and tree care programs designed to enhance long-term urban canopy coverage, prioritizing areas disproportionately impacted by climate change.
Educational Leadership:
  - Operates the Green Team Youth Ambassador Program (GTYA), training at-risk youth, including formerly incarcerated individuals, in environmental science, tree equity, and vegetation management. Participants gain hands-on experience in tree planting, wildfire prevention, and air quality improvement, culminating in pre-apprentice job placements.
  - Provides community education programs on the role of urban forestry in climate adaptation, public health, and environmental justice, emphasizing actionable strategies for local resilience.
Collaborative Partnerships:
  - Works with the Fresno Metropolitan Black Chamber of Commerce, CAL FIRE, PG\&E, and the Central California Environmental Justice Network (CCEJN) to advance environmental justice and sustainability goals.
  - Facilitates cross-sector collaborations to align greening efforts with regional climate resilience strategies, leveraging local air quality data to prioritize high-need areas for intervention.
  - Partnered with CCEJN’s Urban Greening Coordinator to implement the Adopt A Tree program, providing free drought-tolerant trees to eligible households in South Central Fresno.
Community Engagement:
  - Regularly hosts tree planting and greening events across urban neighborhoods, rural areas, and the Sierra Nevada foothills, focusing on fostering community participation and long-term stewardship.
  - Actively involves residents in greening projects, ensuring that community voices shape the planning and implementation processes.
  - Engages underserved populations through equity-driven initiatives, offering direct benefits like shade, improved air quality, and localized cooling to communities most affected by extreme heat.
**Role on Project  
**Mona Cummings and Tree Fresno will provide targeted support to the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan by contributing their knowledge, network, and expertise in urban greening:
**Strategic Targeting of Cities and Sites:**
  - Recommend cities and neighborhoods to target for greening interventions, drawing from their extensive regional knowledge and past collaborations.
  - Provide connections to key parks and public works contacts in those locations, facilitating partnerships and expediting project planning.
**Community Meeting Participation:**
  - Attend community meetings in the five selected target areas to serve as a subject matter expert on tree planting and urban forestry.
  - Answer community questions about tree care, urban greening benefits, and implementation strategies, ensuring the plans reflect both technical expertise and local needs.
**Feedback on Greening Concepts:**
  - Review and provide input on proposed greening concepts for the target neighborhoods, leveraging their hands-on experience and understanding of local environmental conditions.

## Good Faith Efforts Documentation
In addition to Sheila Hakimipour, we reached out to JLB Traffic Engineering (NAICS 541330)** **to do a traffic study but they were not available to take on the work.  
Using the Caltrans database, we were not able to find any Fresno-based DBEs listed under Landscaping Services (NAICS 561730), Landscape Architectural Services (NAICS 541320), Nursery and Tree Production (NAICS 111421), Support Activities for Forestry such as forest management planning, pest control, and tree evaluation (NAICS 115310), nor Translation Services (NAICS 541930). 
Despite not finding any local DBEs for translation services we made efforts to reach out to companies that would maximize local participation.
To that end, we reached out to All Languages Translation Services with no response. They are a Fresno-based minority-owned small business (DUNS: 080153238; CAGE: 7LAL6). Our calls to Fresno Interpreting and Translating were also not returned.  
 
Hyphae has a long history of seeking out local partnerships in the communities we work with and have demonstrated that through our collaboration with BOCC and TF in this proposal, in addition to UDD. See attachment for UDD’s DBE information.

# J. <comment_start id=kix.17hf68sr7vs1>Summary of Qualifications<comment_end id=kix.17hf68sr7vs1>
## Firm Experience
### **Hyphae Design Lab has extensive experience delivering innovative, data-driven solutions that address urban resilience, environmental justice, and pollution mitigation. By integrating technical rigor with robust community engagement, Hyphae empowers communities with actionable data and scalable design strategies tailored to their specific needs.**
  - **Prescott Greening Project **(2021-2024, AB 617 and Transformative Climate Communities funding):
This ongoing project is led by the West Oakland Environmental Indicators Project with Hyphae support. Part of the West Oakland Community Air Action Plan (WOCAP) under AB 617, the project received initial funding from a $150,000 Transformative Climate Communities grant by the California Strategic Growth Council. Additional funding includes $300,000 from the Metropolitan Transportation Commission's Priority Conservation Areas program, matched with $600,000 from Alameda County Transportation Commission, $50,000 from the Port of Oakland, and $100,000 from CalFire. The project integrates air quality monitoring, vegetated buffers, and tree planting to reduce pollution exposure in West Oakland's Prescott neighborhood, the highest pollution hotspot in the region. It employs phased implementation, beginning with a vegetated buffer pilot along Frontage Road, to create scalable solutions for pollution mitigation​
  - **Richmond Parkway Urban Greening Project** (2019-2022, Urban Greening Grant Program):
This project, funded by California’s Urban Greening Grant Program, addresses environmental justice issues in Richmond by enhancing the Richmond Parkway. Key interventions include tree planting, bike lane redesign, and pedestrian access improvements to buffer neighborhoods like Atchison Village and the Iron Triangle from pollution and traffic hazards. The project integrates technical design, community engagement, and job training through partnerships with Groundwork Richmond. Its innovative “Treefense” strategy involves planting dense vegetative barriers to reduce pollution exposure, enhance safety, and improve thermal comfort, leveraging $184,600 in matching funding from the City of Richmond for maintenance and additional in-kind support from CalFire and local stakeholders​.
  - **Stockton AB 617 Urban Forestry and Vegetation Barrier Project** (2021–2025, $2 million):
This multi-year initiative, funded through AB 617 and California’s Transformative Climate Communities (TCC) program, focuses on enhancing Stockton's green infrastructure to mitigate air pollution and urban heat impacts. Key activities include planting and maintaining over 1,750 trees within disadvantaged neighborhoods, creating vegetative barriers to shield residents from pollution, and implementing a prioritization plan for future greening efforts. Collaborating with Little Manila Rising (LMR), the project integrates advanced modeling techniques, such as wind flow and heat island simulations, with community-led outreach to identify sensitive receptors and optimize planting locations. The project’s deliverables include a long-term stewardship master plan and policy recommendations to ensure sustainability beyond the grant period​
  - **<comment_start id=kix.v1wmdhz6ld01>LA Depave Pla<comment_end id=kix.v1wmdhz6ld01>n**  
      
    Partnering with Los Angeles County, Hyphae sourced and compiled data from over 20 agencies to create a countywide green infrastructure strategy. This comprehensive plan integrated GIS layers for land use, transportation, and climate factors to prioritize multi-benefit solutions addressing urban heat mitigation, stormwater management, and depaving opportunities. By overlaying environmental and social vulnerability data, the strategy emphasized interventions in heat-vulnerable areas to maximize cooling effects and enhance community resilience. The initiative was part of a larger Community Forest Management Plan funded by CAL FIRE’s Urban and Community Forestry Program, with an award of $1.5 million.
  - **Oakland STEP Project** (2023–2026, STEP Grant)  
      
    The Oakland STEP project integrated high-resolution parcel-level and meter-scale analyses, synthesizing remote sensing and political data to prioritize interventions for mitigating urban heat. Streetlight data for vehicle and active transportation guided the development of weighting systems that normalized diverse datasets and incorporated environmental justice and community feedback. These systems informed the equitable prioritization of streets and sidewalks for greening, focusing on heat mitigation. Hyphae collaborated with city DOT staff to improve engineering workflows for sidewalks and streetscapes, developing novel tree planting guidelines and protocols for tree well cutouts to enhance cooling and shading in historically neglected areas.  
  - **Southwestern Medical District Streetscape** (Dallas, TX)  
      
    Collaborated with the Texas Trees Foundation to transform the Harry Hines corridor into a multimodal, tree-lined parkway. Using a combination of environmental sensors and agent-based modeling, the project assessed heat stress and air quality impacts. Design solutions prioritized pedestrian thermal comfort and health-driven outcomes, validated through community engagement and site-specific data. Funded by multiple streams, including Texas Trees Foundation and other local agencies, the project integrates evidence-based methodologies to optimize urban resilience and public health benefits
  - **NSF SBIR Phase I and II Grants: AdaptOS Development **(2020–Present)  
      
    Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform, developed with funding from NSF SBIR Phase I and II grants, enables community-based organizations (CBOs) and municipal partners to address environmental risks such as extreme heat, air pollution, and flooding with precision and scalability. This platform bridges gaps in traditional environmental planning by combining hyperlocal data collection, modeling, and design to ensure interventions are both cost-effective and impactful.
The AdaptOS framework is iterative, cycling through measurement, simulation, implementation, and assessment. Modeling is at the core of this process, serving two essential functions. First, it optimizes sensor placement by predicting pollutant dispersion patterns, ensuring accurate monitoring and data collection. Second, it allows communities to simulate the effects of proposed interventions—such as urban greening, truck rerouting, and shade structures—before implementation, providing clarity on which solutions will yield the greatest impact and benefit-to-cost ratio.
This approach addresses a persistent challenge faced by many environmental initiatives, as exemplified by Hyphae’s work on the West Oakland Community Air Action Plan (WOCAP). While WOCAP mandated significant regulatory concessions and outlined 89 pollution mitigation strategies, decision-makers lacked the empirical tools to determine which interventions would have the most significant impact at the hyperlocal level. AdaptOS fills this gap, demonstrating how interventions like tree canopy, vegetation, and traffic patterns can be configured to protect environmental health effectively.
By combining rigorous modeling with actionable design strategies, AdaptOS transforms environmental data into solutions tailored to community needs, ensuring measurable public health and environmental benefits. Hyphae has successfully deployed this platform in projects across Oakland, Stockton, and Dallas, delivering scalable frameworks that can guide future environmental resilience efforts.
  - **The Green Heart Project: Pioneering Urban Greening for Public Health** (2018 – Present)  
      
    The Green Heart Project began in 2018 as a collaborative effort between the University of Louisville, The Nature Conservancy, and multiple local and national partners, including Hyphae Design Lab. With funding from the National Institutes of Health (NIH) and support from The Nature Conservancy, the Green Heart Project is the first clinical trial to study the effects of urban greening on air quality and its direct impact on cardiovascular and systemic health.
Key aspects of the project included planting over 8,000 mature evergreen trees and shrubs across a designated four-square-mile area in Louisville, Kentucky. This effort targeted neighborhoods with historically low tree canopy coverage and high exposure to environmental stressors, including air pollution and urban heat.
The project implemented a **quasi-experimental design**:
  - Baseline (2018-2019): Collection of extensive health and environmental data from 745 residents, including biomarker analyses (e.g., high-sensitivity C-reactive protein or hsCRP), air quality measurements, and noise pollution monitoring.
  - Intervention (2019-2020): Large-scale greening, focusing on high-traffic corridors and residential zones.
  - Post-intervention Assessment (2020-Present): Continued monitoring of participants to evaluate health improvements linked to reduced systemic inflammation and improved air quality.
**Impact**:
  - Participants in neighborhoods receiving greenery saw 13–20% lower levels of hsCRP, corresponding to a 10–15% reduction in risks of cardiovascular disease, cancer, and diabetes.
  - Significant reductions in particulate matter (PM2.5) levels and urban noise pollution were documented, highlighting the multi-dimensional benefits of increased urban greenery.
Scalability and Future Research:
The Green Heart Project recently received $4.6 million in renewed funding from the National Institute of Environmental Health Sciences (NIEHS) to expand its research over the next five years. The project will delve deeper into the long-term benefits of urban greening, exploring how increased tree cover affects heat stress, chronic diseases, and healthcare costs.
## Technical Capabilities
### **Hyphae combines cutting-edge computing resources and innovative methodologies to achieve high-resolution data analysis and design:**
  - ### **Expertise in land cover classification and parcel-scale geospatial analysis, creating novel datasets at the meter scale for entire counties.**
  - ### **Advanced computational modeling, including LiDAR and geospatial data processing supported by NSF-funded workflows.**
  - ### **Cloud and in-house GPU servers for processing street view data and developing detailed urban design interventions.**
  - ### **Heat vulnerability modeling using Universal Thermal Climate Index (UTCI) metrics and integration with pedestrian behavior simulations to inform design strategies.**
## Local Knowledge
### **Hyphae has strong connections with local environmental justice coalitions and community-based organizations (CBOs), including the Central Valley-based California Environmental Justice Coalition (CEJC), Valley Improvements Project, Little Manila Rising, and Mayor Rey Leon’s Latino Equity Advocacy and Policy Institute (LEAP). This established network gives Hyphae a significant advantage in understanding regional needs and engaging stakeholders effectively. Current and ongoing collaborations across California provide a head start in developing the dispersed network of engaged community groups essential for the project’s success.**
### **Hyphae’s deep technical expertise, combined with a track record of integrating data with community-driven approaches, positions the firm as a leader in delivering impactful and equitable solutions for Fresno County.**
