## From Green Latinos draft 
NARRATIVE:
Hyphae Design Laboratory approaches urban forestry and green infrastructure through the lens of environmental justice and community empowerment. While conventional urban forest master plans provide valuable highlevel guidance, we specialize in bridging the critical gap between broad planning documents and actual community implementation. Our experience has shown that the greatest challenges often lie not in the technical planning, but in translating these plans into actionable, communityled initiatives that create lasting impact.
Our approach fundamentally reimagines how tree planting and green infrastructure projects are implemented in environmental justice communities. Rather than following the standard model of filling individual tree wells—which requires extensive outreach for minimal return and often stretches community capacity thin—we identify strategic, highimpact opportunities that maximize environmental benefits while minimizing administrative burden. By focusing on locations such as freeway corridors, school zones, and public parks, we can achieve significant tree canopy expansion through fewer administrative touchpoints, allowing communities to plant hundreds of trees through agreements with just a few key stakeholders rather than seeking approval from hundreds of individual property owners.
Central to our methodology is a commitment to building local economic capacity alongside environmental improvements. Instead of centralizing all project management and implementation within nonprofit organizations, we help communities identify and engage local, minorityowned landscaping businesses to perform the work. This approach not only ensures more efficient project execution but also keeps resources within the community, creating a sustainable model where local businesses become longterm partners in maintaining and expanding urban forestry initiatives. When community members see their neighbors and local businesses leading implementation, it strengthens buyin and ensures more effective longterm stewardship.
Our work extends beyond traditional urban forestry to address the diverse environmental justice challenges facing communities. Drawing from our extensive experience with farmworker communities, warehouse districts, and industrial zones, we understand that effective green infrastructure must be tailored to specific community contexts. This might mean designing vegetative buffers for air quality near warehouses, creating protective greenery near pesticideimpacted areas, or developing innovative solutions for extreme heat mitigation. Our tools and approaches are built from the ground up to address these varied challenges, ensuring that each project not only increases green space but also meaningfully addresses critical environmental health concerns.
Through years of handson experience in communities from Oakland to Los Angeles, we've developed a comprehensive strategy that emphasizes coalition building, maintenance planning, and longterm stewardship alongside initial implementation. By focusing on strategic locations, building local capacity, and maintaining a steadfast commitment to environmental justice principles, we help communities transform highlevel plans into tangible environmental improvements that build both ecological and economic resilience.
**From Green Latinos**
**Expertise in Urban Forestry Activities**
Hyphae Design Lab has a strong track record in urban forestry, leveraging expertise in tree inventories, canopy assessments, site preparation, tree planting, and maintenance. We approach each project with a comprehensive understanding of the technical, environmental, and community aspects of urban greening.
**1. Tree Inventory and Canopy Assessment**
  - **Tree Inventory:** We use advanced tools like **TreePlotter** and **ArcGIS Field Maps** to conduct tree inventories, tracking species, health, location, and age of existing trees in urban settings.
  - **Canopy Assessment:** By utilizing **LiDAR** data, including **Leaf Area Index (LAI)** and **Leaf Area Density (LAD)**, we assess canopy cover, which helps prioritize planting areas and measure potential environmental benefits like air quality improvement and temperature reduction.
**2. Site Preparation**
  - **Site Evaluation:** We assess urban sites for tree planting suitability, reviewing soil quality, available space, infrastructure, and potential challenges such as existing utilities. This is aligned with city ordinances (e.g., 811 guidelines) and environmental regulations.
  - **Preparation:** We manage soil preparation, including excavation for **tree wells**, and ensure compliance with city ordinances to ensure longterm tree health. Our team coordinates with municipal agencies to ensure the tree planting follows all regulations.
**3. Tree Planting and Maintenance**
  - **Tree Planting:** Our ISAcertified arborists are trained in proper tree planting techniques, accommodating various types of nursery stock including **ball and burlap**, **container**, and **bare root** trees. We conduct **planting demonstrations** and emphasize the importance of watering, mulching, and pruning.
  - **Maintenance and Stewardship:** We create customized **maintenance plans** for tree stewardship, which outline how trees will be cared for in the long term. These plans are designed to empower community members and organizations to take on stewardship responsibilities.
**4. Workforce Development and Community Engagement**
  - **Youth Training Programs:** We have created **paid youth tree programs** targeting high school and college students from local communities. Youth participants receive training in **tree planting, watering, pruning, species identification**, and **tree health assessments**. Additionally, they learn about orchard care, community outreach, and the importance of urban trees.
  - **Adult Leader Programs:** We provide **paid stipends** for adult leaders who assist with community engagement, organizing **tree planting events**, **giveaways**, and **greening projects**. These leaders also liaise with city officials, ensuring that community needs are represented.
  - **Community Events:** Hyphae has hosted **biannual tree planting events** and **fruit tree giveaways**, offering an opportunity for local residents to get involved and learn about urban forestry.
Community Engagement Strategies for Urban Forestry
1\. **Outreach Strategies**
  - **Community Surveys and Social Media:** We assess the preferred communication methods in each community (surveys, social media, radio, or community events) and tailor our outreach accordingly.
  - **Feedback Loops:** We implement systems like **suggestion boxes** or online platforms for ongoing community feedback, ensuring that the community’s voice is heard throughout the project. This is a valuable tool to gauge satisfaction and identify areas for improvement.
**2. Program Assessment and Goal Setting**
  - **Review Past Programs:** By evaluating previous tree programs, we determine what worked well and where there were challenges. This helps in setting realistic goals for new tree planting initiatives.
  - **Collaborative Goal Setting:** Community input is integral in shaping program goals, ensuring they reflect the local needs, such as increasing canopy cover, improving air quality, or fostering workforce development.
**3. Community Leadership Development**
  - **Stipended Leaders:** We work to develop a group of **community leaders** who can advocate for the project, organize events, and engage neighbors. This model encourages communitydriven stewardship.
  - **Mapping and Citizen Science:** By combining **community mapping projects** with citizen science efforts, we help communities identify priority planting locations, such as schools, bus stops, and gathering areas.
In collaboration with **GreenLatinos' Justicia Y Equidad Grants Manager**, we ensure transparent and efficient budget management.
## Task 6:  Develop Planning Level Costs for the Identified Shade Improvement Projects
  - Develop planning level costs for each of the identified tree canopy and vegetative cover projects. 
Deliverables: Planning Level Costs for Identified Shade Improvement Projects
## Task 7: Select Five Projects in the Most Vulnerable Areas and Develop Project Profiles
  - Working with the working group, identify five projects in the most vulnerable areas. 
  - Develop vulnerability analysis project profiles that describe neighborhood overview, extreme heat vulnerability analysis scores, physical and socioeconomic impacts of extreme heat, and costbenefit analysis of implementing the shade adaptation project, and fact sheets for the five selected projects.
Deliverables: 
Criteria for Project Selection 
Project Profiles for Five Projects 
Project Fact Sheets for Five Projects
## Task 8: Develop the Draft Plan and Publish the Draft for Public Review
  - Develop the draft plan that includes recommendations for short, medium, and longterm strategies for implementation and maintenance of projects for review from FCOG and the working group. The strategies should consider active transportation and public transit and should have cobenefits to public health, natural ecosystems, social equity, the economy, and greenhouse gas emissions. 
  - Publish the plan for 30day public review.
Deliverables: Draft Plan and Public Comments
## Task 9: Presentation and Policy Board Acceptance
  - Present the draft final plan to the Transportation Technical Committee, the Policy Advisory Committee, and the Policy Board for acceptance.
  - Incorporate comments from the committees into the final plan
Deliverables: 
PowerPoint Presentation 
Final Plan

From Marin\_CARB 5
Hyphae design lab (ecological design and engineering) staff
#### **Principal ($200/hr)**
Brent Bucknum is an ecologist and executive with decades of experience facilitating discussion between community based organizations and technical organizations. His focused involvement on this project will be critical to shaping the direction and strategy of project goals and outcomes.
#### **Designer & Project Manager ($150/hr)**
Mei Visco is a project manager and ecological designer with years of experience in large grantfunded environmental justice projects in Oakland, Louisville, and beyond. Her specific skillsets include interfacing with activist and governmental organizations on complex and controversial environmental issues while persistently advancing project tasks. 
#### **Technical Design lead ($200/hr)**
Ivan Heitmann, MLA, is a landscape designer with extensive experience in computational environmental modeling. His understanding of the mechanics of green infrastructure and design process as well as familiarity with rigorous simulation procedures make him core to the implementation of this project. His experience with other granted projects with related scopes in Marin City and around the bay ensure his ability to move this project forward. 
#### **Modeling specialist ($150/hr)**
Juan Penaloza Gutierrez, PhD, is an expert in computational fluid dynamics. His technical mastery of the mathematics and protocols underpinning hyphae’s environmental simulation efforts are fundamental to the execution of the work plan.
**Section 1: Key Personnel**
Merissa MacDonald  ISA PD3010A
Juan PenalozaGutierrez, PhD
Mei Visco, Ecological Designer
Brent Bucknum, Founder and Principal
**Merissa MacDonald, ISACertified Arborist and Urban Forestry Specialist, Project Manager  PD3010A**
**Current Position**: Arborist and Partnerships Manager, Hyphae Design Lab
  
Merissa MacDonald is a certified arborist with deep experience in urban forestry, coalitionbuilding, and communityled green infrastructure projects. Her previous role as Tree Program Coordinator at Bartram’s Garden in Philadelphia showcases her success in fostering environmental equity and community engagement in Southwest Philadelphia. Merissa continues to support this program as a consultant, providing her expertise and local insight as a former neighbor.
At Bartram’s Garden, Merissa created and led the Southwest Tree Tenders group, securing funding from the Knight Foundation and the Pennsylvania Horticultural Society (PHS) to plant over 400 trees in underserved neighborhoods. She developed an internship program, the Southwest Tree Crew, which trained and employed up to 16 local high school students each year. Through this, Merissa empowered youth to become certified PHS Tree Tenders and advocates for environmental justice, training them in arboricultural practices, public speaking, and workforce development skills.
Merissa's leadership extended to founding the Southwest Tree Coalition, a network that unites community members, artists, gardeners, and landscape architects to enhance tree planting efforts. Under her direction, the coalition built a strong community presence through social media, branding, and highprofile planting events, including the annual SpringFest event, which attracted over 2,000 participants.
**Specific Responsibilities and Role within Hyphae Design Lab**:
  - **Partnerships Management**: Cultivates and manages relationships with key stakeholders, ranging from longstanding collaborators to new alliances, to strengthen community forestry and workforce development initiatives.
  - **Technical Arboricultural Support**: Provides expertise and guidance for urban forestry projects, advising on tree planting, maintenance, and canopy restoration efforts.
  - **Grant Development**: Supports grant writing by identifying funding opportunities, contributing technical content, and organizing proposal materials to enhance project funding success.
  - **Community Engagement and Outreach**: Leads outreach and communications efforts to promote program goals, sharing resources and insights through events, presentations, and partnerships.
  - **Workforce Development**: Designs training programs and resources to upskill individuals in urban forestry practices, with a focus on engaging underserved and bilingual communities.
Merissa’s background includes extensive work with lowcanopy, environmentally impacted areas, especially with underserved and bilingual (African Frenchspeaking) communities. Her experience leading inclusive, communitybased environmental programs has fostered strong connections and lasting impact in Southwest Philadelphia. Through her role at Hyphae, Merissa continues to advocate for communityled, equitable green infrastructure solutions tailored to address the unique needs of disadvantaged communities.
**Juan Peñaloza Gutierrez, PhD  
Role**: CFD Specialist  
**Position**: Software Engineer (CFD Modeler), Hyphae Design Laboratory
**  
**Dr. Juan Peñaloza Gutierrez brings advanced expertise in Computational Fluid Dynamics (CFD) to his role as a Software Engineer at Hyphae Design Lab. With a PhD in Mechanical Engineering from the University of South Florida, Juan specializes in turbulence modeling, pollution dispersion, and air quality simulations in complex urban environments. His research includes advanced simulations, such as ReynoldsAveraged NavierStokes (RANS) and LargeEddy Simulations (LES), applied to pollution transport, windfield analysis, and aerosol dispersion in spaces like hospital isolation rooms. Juan has presented his work at leading conferences, including the Ocean Sciences Meeting and the Annual APS Division of Fluid Dynamics Meeting.
At Hyphae Design Lab, Juan’s primary role involves using CFD to model air pollution dispersion and assess the effectiveness of green infrastructure in mitigating air quality issues. His responsibilities include:
  - **CFD Model Development and Application**: Developing highresolution RANS and LES models to simulate pollutant dispersion around urban green infrastructure and examining the influence of natural barriers on air quality.
  - **Project Analysis and Reporting**: Providing datadriven insights to support environmental designs and interventions, refining models to address projectspecific environmental challenges.
  - **Technical Consultation**: Advising teams on the integration of CFD results with ecological and urban design strategies, ensuring project goals align with local environmental needs.
**  
**As a bilingual engineer, Juan offers accessibility in Spanishspeaking communities, helping bridge technical language barriers in underserved areas. His work at Hyphae Design Lab often involves collaborating with local stakeholders to make complex CFD insights understandable for residents and community groups, particularly those in areas affected by industrial pollution. This approach allows him to tailor his technical expertise in ways that promote inclusive and effective solutions for air quality improvement in underserved urban neighborhoods.
**Mei Visco, Ecological Designer and Project Manager, Hyphae Design Lab**
Mei Visco is an ecological designer and experienced project manager with a focus on urban forestry, air quality improvement, and communityled greening initiatives. Mei has managed several highimpact projects funded by agencies like CARB, SEP, MTC, and CNRA, partnering closely with local communities and city organizations to address environmental justice and promote sustainable urban design.
1.  **STEP Grant Project Manager** (CARB Funded)
      - Mei led this CARBfunded grant project to plant 1,5002,000 trees in West Oakland by collaborating with city partners and local community organizations. She coordinated an implementation plan, data collection, and budgeting to meet grant requirements, prioritizing tree locations based on air pollution data and equity metrics. The West Oakland community, historically underserved, benefits from a longterm stewardship plan Mei developed by engaging local treeplanting groups for resource sharing and maintenance.
      - **Responsibilities**: Project management, implementation and data collection plan development, budget creation and oversight, design and master planning, collaboration with local groups, and stakeholder coordination.
2.  **Prescott Greening Project Manager** (SEP and MTC Funded)
      - Mei managed this tree planting and street redesign project in West Oakland, designed to intercept nearroad air pollution along key transportation corridors. This project addresses pollution sources from highways, railways, and the port.
      - **Responsibilities**: Project management, partnership coordination with the West Oakland Environmental Indicators Project (WOEIP), creation of design charrettes for community input, concept designs for roadway and planting, air pollution simulation modeling management, contractor collaboration, and budget management.
3.  **Richmond Tree Fence Project Manager** (CNRA Urban Greening Grant)
      - Mei partnered with Groundwork Richmond to install a tree barrier along roadways near disadvantaged communities in Richmond. The project captures nearroad pollution while providing green infrastructure benefits to the local area.
      - **Responsibilities**: Site analysis, grant application writing, design development, collaboration with arborists and horticulture experts, budget oversight, contractor coordination for irrigation installation, and grant compliance, including greenhouse gas calculations.
4.  **Green Heart Project Designer** (NIH Funded)
      - This NIHfunded initiative aimed to plant 8,000 trees in a disadvantaged neighborhood in Louisville, Kentucky, and study the impact on local air quality and resident health. Mei was responsible for designing plantings at strategic locations.
      - **Responsibilities**: Sitespecific planting designs, final plant list creation, and collaboration with tree brokers for planting logistics.
**  
**All of Mei’s projects focus on improving conditions in underserved areas. She has extensive experience engaging local communities through presentations, communityled design workshops, and feedback sessions to ensure projects align with residents' needs. Mei emphasizes reinvestment in the community by working with local contractors and allocating stipends for community involvement. Her approach fosters a sense of ownership and sustainability in each project.
Mei’s ability to work collaboratively with community groups, secure local buyin, and create actionable, longterm environmental plans makes her a key asset to Hyphae Design Lab’s mission of communitycentered ecological design.
**Brent Bucknum  
Role**: Founder, Principal  
**Position**: Principal Ecological Designer, Hyphae Design Laboratory
**  
**With over two decades of experience in ecological design and green infrastructure innovation, Brent Bucknum has pioneered numerous environmental justice initiatives as the Principal and Founder of Hyphae Design Lab. His background includes a BA in Ecology and International Environmental Policy from Carleton College, and his expertise spans ecological restoration, green infrastructure, and communitycentered environmental design. As a cofounder of both the Green Heart Project in Louisville and Urban Biofilter in Oakland, Brent has applied ecological design to tackle air, water, and soil pollution, particularly within disadvantaged communities. His research focuses on the development and implementation of green infrastructure technologies, which enhance urban air quality and foster community resilience.
Brent has presented his work at national and international conferences, such as the American Planning Association, the Ecological Society of America, and the American Society of Landscape Architects. He has contributed to urban ecology and public health research, including studies with Dr. Aruni Bhatnagar on the health impacts of green buffers in urban settings.
**Specific Responsibilities and Roles within Projects  
**As Principal at Hyphae Design Lab, Brent oversees project visioning and strategic partnerships, applying ecological principles to urban design. His key responsibilities include:
  - **Project Leadership and Strategic Planning**: Directing projects from concept to execution, particularly those involving green infrastructure and air quality mitigation in highimpact urban areas.
  - **Community Engagement**: Facilitating stakeholder workshops and presentations that communicate scientific concepts in accessible ways, fostering partnerships with local groups and residents.
  - **Research and Innovation**: Leading research initiatives to evaluate and optimize green infrastructure’s effectiveness, including buffer installations for pollutant reduction and ecosystembased urban design.
Brent has extensive experience working with disadvantaged communities, focusing on integrating green infrastructure in areas with limited environmental resources and high pollution exposure. Through projects like the Green Heart Project, he has collaborated with diverse stakeholders to engage communities in meaningful discussions about health and ecological resilience, ensuring that local voices inform each project phase. His work with Urban Biofilter has further demonstrated his commitment to environmental equity, as he advocates for ecological solutions tailored to the unique challenges of underserved urban areas.

## Material from EHCRP Proposal
### Project Vision
The vision of the California Environmental Justice Coalition is to form a
communityled research observatory and task force capable of monitoring
and mitigating the deadly effects of extreme heat in the Central and
Imperial valleys. Our project, the Communityled Microclimate Observation
Network (COMMON), aims to transform these regions into models of
climate resilience, and brings a unique focus on farmworkerpopulated
agricultural areas that are often left out of extreme heat remediation efforts
directed towards cities.
In this endeavor, CEJC has partnered with environmental justice member
organizations with proven records of championing their community’s health
and environment. These organizations, supported by technical partners,
stand to make a historic intervention in the field by correlating hyperlocal
extreme heat data with population and hospital data that will provide
updated heathealth intervention measures, provide better outreach and
early warning systems, and an empirical basis for updating codes,
standards, and regulations that presently fail to protect vulnerable
populations from extreme heat.
As COMMON is designed not only to monitor but also to actively respond
to extreme heat, both with emergency outreach tools and climate
adaptation solutions, its goals of equity, community leadership, and
scientific rigor are in clear alignment with those of the EHCRP.
### Project Description
COMMON aims to enhance the climate resilience of the San Joaquin and
Imperial Valleys with communitydriven solutions. The following 30month
implementation timeline combines communityled outreach, sensor
installation, data collection, software development, and mitigation solutions.
Year 1: Coalitionbuilding and Citizen Science
 CEJC and Partners conduct rigorous community outreach campaigns
across the San Joaquin and Imperial valleys to identify heatvulnerable
neighborhoods.
 Simultaneously, technical Partners from NASA’s Jet Propulsion Lab (JPL),
Hyphae Design Labs (HDL), and UC Merced (UCM) model the area’s
potential highrisk environments. High resolution modeling, usable within
first year of project, provides communities with data to choose where to
deploy sensors, conduct simulations, and plot mitigation strategies. HDL
then trains monitoring technicians of respective Partners on assembly and
installation of open source thermal stress sensors, as well as in use of
Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform to collect and interpret the data.
Year 2 and 3: Immediate Use of Data & Sensor Deployment
 HDL develops an extreme heat early warning and outreach mobile app
capable of alerting residents about heat events and guiding users to
cooling centers and sensitive receptor sites, avoiding hotspots along the
way.
 By Month 24, Partners and HDL have installed sensor network in selected
neighborhoods,
 Partners assist HDL, JPL and UCM in providing communitydriven data
overlay of community’s primary walking and transit routes, gathering
places, and sensitive receptors.
 Hyphae creates high resolution digital twins of geographical areas
prioritized by communities.
 Partners and community finalize targeted greening sites with Tree Fresno.
Greening Projects and Adaptation Reporting
 In year three, installed pilot greening projects are outfitted with sensors to
monitor impact on local microclimates and evaluate their effectiveness as
natural cooling measures.
 Project culminates in report detailing outcomes/efficacy of interventions,
community feedback, and models for expanding COMMON to other regions.
### Project Alignment (w/ California Extreme Heat Action Plan) 
COMMON synthesizes the four objectives of the EHCRP, but with a
particular focus on Track A, Goals 2 and 3: developing better assessment
tools, higher resolution modeling, and data collection methods that improve
heathealth observation.
Our Network builds critical datasets of extreme heat into existing
communityrun, paradigmshifting monitoring networks, including IVAN and
SJV Air, the largest communitymanaged remote sensor network in the
country. By placing the best available science and technology into the
hands of the region’s most culturally competent community leaders,
COMMON’s objectives are actionable from day 1 of our timeline. Our
monitoring and outreach partners are grassroots environmental justice
organizations fighting for decades on behalf of voiceless and marginalized
people. The very language of this application prompt, centering the agency
of the local CBOs themselves over that of outside actors, demonstrates
the success of our coalition’s multidecade advocacy for equitycentered
processes in governmentissued grants.
We are proud that our community efforts have successfully set a precedent
for the State to follow. The AB 617 framework grew out of the ability of our
coalition actors to harness lowcost air quality sensors in service of
community needs. The Transforming Climate Communities and California
Climate Investments funding streams, as well as the governmentto
government and Community Air grants, from US EPA and CARB
respectively, also resulted from this communitydriven process. Our
platform adds geographicallyspecific early heat warning systems to these
air quality programs, underscoring heat’s economic impact while empirically
justifying the need for statewide syndromic surveillance in the State’s
hospital system.
### Community Needs and Priorities:
Though each of the subcontracted CEJC partners fights its own unique
pollution challenges, we are all united in the fight against extreme heat. In
order to adequately assess the vulnerability of our respective communities,
however, we require data that has enough granularity to a) pinpoint high
risk areas and b) correlate that vulnerability with health outcomes. With this
hyperlocal data provided by our technical partners, we can then direct
decisionmakers to implement projects, codes, standards, regulations, and
policies that protect our populations from extreme heat.
Based on a combination of remote sensing data, environmental
simulations, and communityplaced heat stress sensors, the community will
use Hyphae’s AdaptOS platform to generate meterscale thermal stress
metrics. These calculations first integrate 70 meter regional ECOSTRESS
satellite surface temperature data, weather data, and GoogleStreetview
derived skyview/shadefactor imagery for each region. Based on this heat
data, as well as on an assessment of sensitive receptors and mitigation
potential, CEJC’s collaborative process produces a firstlevel prioritization
of target neighborhoods. HDL will then generate a digital twin from LIDAR
data at the 1 meter scale, which will enable hyperlocal environmental
simulations of heat stress in the target neighborhoods. These metrics are
generated iteratively as the community discusses prioritization and
mitigation strategies to adaptively optimize the priority locations based on
heat stress potential (temperature, population, sensitive receptors),
mitigation potential (suitability for addition of cooling infrastructure or route
planning), and logistics (mitigation costs). Community members will place
thermal stress sensors in the target neighborhoods to validate the
simulations. A cohort of volunteers will also be outfitted with mobile sensors
to track thermal stress throughout the activity spaces of the community.
Trusted community leaders then circulate through the chosen
neighborhoods to confirm that the gathering places of its most vulnerable
inhabitants are carefully mapped out and included in the models.
### CommunityInformed Project Design:
COMMON centers the agency and needs of Central and Imperial valley
communities, many of whom are outdoor workers lacking tree cover and
public shade, and who suffer from poverty, historic trauma, high power
costs, and lack of transportation.
Project Design
COMMON sustains Partner legacies of communitydriven design by
deploying sensor networks, an early warning mobile app, and interventions
designed to mitigate extreme heat. Sensor networks link realtime data to
the mobile app, thus leveraging HDL’s existing environmental simulation
technologies (funded by the National Science Foundation) to alert residents
about heat events and the safest, coolest routes to their destinations.
Personal Heat Sensors are given to workers and heat mitigation projects
(including but not limited to greening) are designed by the community based
on datainformed community feedback. Sensor data and simulations are
then used to validate the efficacy of these mitigation projects.
Community Input
1\. Design begins with community meetings and various outreach
strategies, with a focus on community mapping to gather firsthand accounts
of heat impact and potential solutions.
2\. Continuous feedback gathered from community and data refinement
determines sensor location, app function, and selection of adaptation
areas.
3\. Partner groups are trained to manage sensor network and develop
Capacity.
### Extreme Heat Conditions and Vulnerability:
If greenhouse gas emissions continue to rise at current rates, the
temperature in downtown Fresno will hit 106.6 fahrenheit 43 times a year.
Classified as Heat Health Events (HHEs), studies have predicted that
these spikes in heat could last up to two weeks long in our region (Thorne
et al., 2018). Adapting to these conditions and durations will require large
scale targeted neighborhood community interventions.Though the risk to
urban environments is wellknown, COMMON also seeks to draw attention
to the less widely understood Rural Heat Island Effect (RHIE).
There exists an abundance of federal, state and local public health
information on extreme heat from fact sheets, websites, and checklists, but
these sources often provide only general guidelines, with broad county
level data. Tools like Caladapt use downscaled daily CMIP5 Climate
Projections but are also at a resolution of 3 miles, so entire cities are
sometimes represented as a single data point. Moreover, existing heat
warning metrics that measure only temperature and humidity fail to
accurately record critical thermal comfort variables such as wind flow,
radiant temperature and land use, that significantly drive heat stress.
These tools also lack critical, subjective, community driven data of how and
where people live, work, gather and move. Therefore the existing heat
awareness tools are inadequate to target impacts and generate effective
solutions. To more accurately warn the public about heat risk, microclimate
variables need to be integrated into community models forecasting and
response services also need to be updated to take into account diverse
populations. Community driven population data can also help improve
healthcare response to target the ripple effect, through which extreme heat
renders the public more vulnerable to mental health emergencies and
chronic health conditions from migraine and rosacea, to lupus and
rheumatoid arthritis, cardiovascular disease, respiratory distress, and
kidney failure.
### How are individuals in heat vulnerable communities represented and given power in the decisionmaking processes of the grant?
In COMMON’s design and execution, individuals from heatvulnerable
communities are not only represented but are in control of the grant and
central to decisionmaking process. This project leverages existing, long
established EJ community groups to further empower their communities
through heat information. These community groups are directly funded to
lead the direction of the grant. Through the coalition structure. These
community groups create structure and assume the leadership roles in both
planning and implementation phases of the grant. COMMON partners have
already been conducting extensive climate mitigation community outreach,
including town hall meetings, polls and surveys, focus groups, flyering,
doortodoor canvassing, social media posts, and email blasts to ensure
that the project's priorities align with the actual needs of the local residents
through TCC and other grants. These long term, real engagements allow for
our coalition to identify key heatvulnerable areas and this critical data to
the dialogue and have the community inform the placement and types of
interventions, such as sensor locations, cooling centers and heat
mitigation strategies like greening projects. Community members are
trained to install, manage, and interpret data from the heat sensors,
fostering not only involvement but also ownership and skill development.
### Explain barriers to applying with a local and/or regional public entity
The coalition has intentionally proposed a communitybased project which
precludes a public entity as coapplicant. But we do intend to work with
agencies and public landowners in our process.
A common barrier to placing sensor networks and planting trees is in
securing both permits from local governments for public property
placements and buyin from local property owners to place materials on
their property. In our case, however, coalition Partners have secured both
the consent of local property owners and signed agreements with local
governments for tree planting. Partners have already installed air pollution
sensors throughout their respective regions. We can add heat sensors to
those same locations without incurring additional overhead for site
logistics. Meanwhile Tree Fresno’s existing agreements with local
governments allow ongoing treeplanting efforts. To demonstrate this, we
have included letters from Caltrans, the City of Fresno, and schools
showing ongoing support for Tree Fresno work.
### Engagement: How you will engage with local and regional public entities and/or
### tribes throughout the project?
As described above, our choice to propose a communitybased project with
no public entity as coapplicant is first a practical decision. But it is also a
choice rooted in our coalition’s historical trauma as migrant farmworkers
and survivors of redlined communities. Regarding air quality in particular,
many of our members are reluctant to share data with the State, especially
if it could impact their property value.
We eagerly hope to add native tribes into our coalition in future rounds of
funding, however. We have been in talks with Mariko Falke, the Executive
Tribal Liaison of California’s Department of Water Resources (DWR),
about future opportunities for partnership, as well as with Desiree Ramirez,
of DWR’s Financial Assistance Branch (FAB). Though these talks revealed
a shared commitment to address the overlapping issues of heat, drought,
and water rights, they also impressed upon us the value of adopting a long
term trustbuilding approach in forming partnerships with groups that share
their own privacy concerns and justifiable ambivalence towards State
actors. As tribal governments are not topdown institutions, they
meticulously consult the whole community before embarking partnerships.
### Partnership Development
As COMMON evolves, we fully intend to partner with tribes and local public
authorities, as well as government agencies and public landowners. Though
this phase of COMMON’s evolution does not propose naturebased
solutions on public property, we hope that in the future expansion of the
Observation Network, we can secure public cooperation on a number of
large implementation projects installed on public rightofways. These
include installing green corridors along public transportation routes, “green
oases” protecting mobile home parks populated by farmworkers, and “cool
groves” that provide shade in agricultural hotspots.
### Describe how the proposed project will prioritize strategies and outcomes that provide climate change adaptation cobenefits, especially for the benefit of vulnerable communities?
The proposed extreme heat monitoring and mitigation network offers a
multifaceted approach to addressing the challenges posed by climate
change, particularly for vulnerable communities. By generating hyperlocal
predictions of biophysical heat stress and providing a userfriendly app, the
project empowers community members to navigate extreme heat events
safely. The installation of thermal stress sensors in neighborhoods and the
provision of mobile sensors to farmworkers and other atrisk populations,
when combined with health data from UCM will generate valuable data to
better understand the impact of extreme heat on public health outcomes
and healthcare costs. .
This project prioritizes social equity by focusing on environmental justice
communities who are on the frontlines of public health challenges related to
climate change. The data collected will be used to inform public policy,
codes, standards, and regulations, particularly in protecting workers from
extreme heat conditions. By quantifying the economic impact of extreme
heat on the healthcare system, the project will draw attention to the urgent
need for heat mitigation strategies and health disparities caused by
extreme heat's disproportionate impact on marginalized communities.
Furthermore, the project will create jobs in sensor fabrication and LIDAR
driving, supporting the local economy while improving transportation
infrastructure through the development of a routing app that minimizes heat
stress for pedestrians. By associating heat events with hospital
admissions data, it will become possible to code heatrelated illnesses into
hospital admissions procedures, and by combining hospital admissions
data with hyperlocal temperature data, the project will provide a more
accurate picture of the health risks and healthcare costs associated with
extreme heat, informing tailored interventions for various populations,
including athletes, firefighters, and outdoor workers.
Overall, this project offers a comprehensive approach to climate change
adaptation, delivering social equity, public health, economic, and
environmental cobenefits.
## Material from EHCRP Interview Process 
The project's technical approach represents a significant advancement beyond conventional heat mapping methods, addressing the fundamental limitation of traditional data sources like EcoStress and CalAdapt that lack sufficient resolution for communitylevel action. The approach integrates multiple sophisticated data sources, beginning with Local Climate Zone (LCZ) classification that provides detailed meteorological land use categories spanning farmland, city areas, and varying densities of subdivisions. This classification is enhanced by two innovative population mapping systems: Harvard's Popgrid, which utilizes Microsoft's building data layer to distribute population estimates at the building level rather than census tract, and RTI's synthetic populations system, which applies mathematical approaches to distribute demographic data with unprecedented precision.
The heart of the technical approach lies in its sophisticated modeling methodology. The team builds detailed simulation models for each neighborhood, incorporating wind data to calculate thermal comfort metrics. The Universal Thermal Climate Index measurements integrate air temperature, radiant temperature, humidity, and wind data to create a comprehensive understanding of how temperature is actually experienced by residents. This approach achieves square meterlevel resolution, producing something akin to wet bulb temperature calculations but at a much more granular level than previously possible. These models are specifically calibrated for and run in each focus community.
What makes this approach particularly powerful is its practical applicability. The modeling can identify temperature variations of 2030 degrees within distances as short as 2030 feet, helping communities identify immediate solutions even before interventions are implemented. Unlike traditional heat mapping that might simply show an area as a "red spot," this approach can pinpoint specific cool areas, such as those near creeks or under tree canopy. This is especially effective in the Central Valley's dry climate. The models are validated through strategically placed sensors that measure temperature, humidity, and globe temperature, creating wet bulb globe temperature equivalents at the meter level.
Perhaps most importantly, the technical approach is deeply integrated with community knowledge and needs. Models are developed in continuous dialogue with communities, incorporating hyperlocal demographic and behavioral input  such as identifying frequently used bus stops or gathering spaces. This integration helps prioritize intervention locations and provides immediately actionable information for community members. The approach effectively avoids the paralysis often induced by traditional "doom and gloom" heat mapping by identifying specific, achievable improvements. Rather than presenting communities with overwhelming, tractlevel heat data, it provides meterlevel resolution that's particularly wellsuited for rural and mixeduse communities. This combination of technical sophistication and community integration creates a powerful tool for understanding and addressing urban heat islands at a truly local level.
1.  **Bulletpoint form**:
1.  Data Sources and Resolution
    1.  Moving beyond conventional heat data sources (EcoStress, CalAdapt)
    2.  Key issue: Traditional sources lack sufficient resolution
    3.  Using multiple enhanced data sources: a. Local Climate Zones (LCZ) Classification
        1.  Detailed meteorological land use classification
        2.  Categories include farmland, city, open/dense subdivisions
        3.  More accurate than traditional classifications b. Population Data Integration:
        4.  Popgrid (Harvard)  Uses buildinglevel data
        5.  Microsoft building data layer integration
        6.  Distributes population estimates to actual buildings rather than census tracts
        7.   More precise than traditional census data c. RTI Synthetic Populations
        8.  Demographic data distributed to building locations
        9.  Uses mathematical approaches for greater precision
        10. Particularly useful for health data analysis
2.  Modeling Approach
    1.  Building simulation models for each neighborhood
    2.  Incorporates wind data for thermal comfort calculations
    3.  Creates Universal Thermal Climate Index measurements including:
        1.  Air temperature
        2.  Radiant temperature
        3.  Humidity
        4.  Wind
    4.  Achieves square meterlevel resolution for communities
    5.  Similar to wet bulb temperature but more granular
    6.  Models run specifically in focus communities.
3.  Practical Applications
    1.  Identifies temperature variations within small areas (2030 degree differences within 2030 feet)
    2.  Helps identify immediate solutions before interventions
    3.  More actionable than traditional "red spot" mapping
    4.  Can identify specific cool areas (e.g., near creeks, under trees)
    5.  Particularly effective in Central Valley's dry climate
4.  Validation Method
    1.  Deploying sensors to validate models
    2.  Sensors measure multiple variables:
        1.  Temperature
        2.  Humidity
        3.  Globe temperature
    3.  Creates wet bulb globe temperature equivalents
    4.  Validation at meterlevel resolution
5.  Community Integration
    1.  Models developed in dialogue with communities
    2.  Gathers hyperlocal demographic and behavioral input
    3.   Identifies specific community usage patterns (e.g., bus stop locations)
    4.  Helps prioritize intervention locations
    5.  Provides immediate actionable information for community members
6.  Advantages Over Traditional Approaches
    1.  Avoids "doom and gloom" paralysis of traditional heat mapping
    2.  Identifies specific actionable areas for improvement
    3.  Provides meterlevel resolution versus census tract data
    4.  Better suited for rural and mixeduse communities
    5.  Integrates community knowledge with technical data
This technical approach represents a significant advancement over traditional heat mapping methods, providing more detailed, actionable data while incorporating community needs and behaviors.
5\) How to present the Workplan?
  - Pick a clear framing: Our workplan accomplishes 4 things: list them and then provide bullet points for each category. The idea is to not get lost in the details and present it to OPR synthetically.
  - Here’s a potential way to frame it:
Our workplan accomplishes 4 key things:
1\. Data Collection & Analysis
  - Building a comprehensive sensor network across communities
  - Integrating groundlevel monitoring with advanced modeling
  - Creating digital twins for hyperlocal heat mapping
  - Combining multiple data sources (community sensors, satellite data, health data)
2\. Community Engagement & Training
  - Training community organizations in sensor deployment and data interpretation
  - Hosting community meetings to determine sensor placement and interventions
  - Developing culturally competent outreach strategies
  - Building capacity for longterm community science
3\. Technology Development & Implementation
  - Creating an extreme heat routing and notification app
  - Developing userfriendly data visualization tools
  - Integrating realtime data with predictive modeling
  - Building decisionsupport tools for communities
4\. NatureBased Solutions
  - Implementing targeted greening interventions
  - Installing pilot projects with monitoring capabilities
  - Measuring intervention effectiveness
  - Creating sustainable maintenance plans
This structure shows how the project moves from data collection through community engagement to actual interventions, while building sustainable infrastructure for future expansion.
Each component has clear deliverables, involves multiple partners, and builds toward the project's ultimate goal of creating a replicable model for communityled climate adaptation.
From Claude
Executive Summary:
The entire executive summary could be adapted to provide a highlevel overview of the Fresno project, highlighting the key objectives, approach, and anticipated outcomes.
Simulation:
The "Role of ENVImet" section could be repurposed to describe Hyphae's technical capabilities and the value of advanced modeling techniques for the Fresno project.
The "Inputs," "Outputs and implementation," and "Design Process, Collaboration and Iteration" sections provide valuable details on data requirements, expected deliverables, and stakeholder engagement that could be incorporated into the relevant sections of the Fresno proposal.
Monitoring and prepost study:
The overview of the monitoring approach and key findings from the CAMO project could be adapted to strengthen the case for the proposed monitoring and evaluation activities in Fresno.
Methods:
The entire Methods section, including subsections on Simulation, Design Process, and Monitoring, could be repurposed to provide a detailed description of Hyphae's technical approach and methodologies for the Fresno project.
Specific elements like the digital twin procedure, CFD meshing, meteorological inputs, traffic data integration, and simulation output metrics are directly relevant to the proposed vulnerability analysis and intervention design tasks.
The collaboration and iteration process outlined in the CAMO proposal aligns well with the stakeholder engagement and participatory design approach envisioned for the Fresno project.
Results:
While the specific results from the CAMO project may not be directly applicable, the structure and format of this section could be adapted to present the anticipated outcomes and deliverables for the Fresno project.
The subsections on thermal comfort simulation, pollution modeling, and monitoring results demonstrate Hyphae's ability to translate complex technical analyses into meaningful, actionable insights for decisionmakers and community stakeholders.
Appendices:
The literature review and annotated bibliography could be repurposed to demonstrate Hyphae's deep domain expertise and familiarity with best practices in urban heat mitigation, air quality monitoring, and naturebased solutions.
The glossary of terms and definitions could be adapted to ensure clarity and consistency in the Fresno proposal.
By systematically reviewing the CAMO proposal and identifying all the relevant sections, we can ensure that the Fresno RFP response leverages the full breadth of Hyphae's experience, technical capabilities, and proven methodologies. This comprehensive approach will result in a stronger, more compelling proposal that demonstrates Hyphae's unique qualifications and value proposition for the Fresno Extreme Heat project.
Here are the key sections from the CAMO proposal that can be adapted for the Fresno Extreme Heat RFP, along with notes on where they might fit:
Project Vision (for the Project Overview section):
"The vision of the California Environmental Justice Coalition is to form a communityled research observatory and task force capable of monitoring and mitigating the deadly effects of extreme heat in the Central and Imperial valleys. Our project, the Communityled Microclimate Observation Network (COMMON), aims to transform these regions into models of climate resilience, and brings a unique focus on farmworkerpopulated agricultural areas that are often left out of extreme heat remediation efforts directed towards cities."
Project Description (for the Project Overview section):
"COMMON aims to enhance the climate resilience of the San Joaquin and Imperial Valleys with communitydriven solutions. The following 30month implementation timeline combines communityled outreach, sensor installation, data collection, software development, and mitigation solutions."
(The detailed 3year timeline that follows could be adapted to fit the Fresno project scope and deliverables)
Community Needs and Priorities (for the Communities Served section):
"Though each of the subcontracted CEJC partners fights its own unique pollution challenges, we are all united in the fight against extreme heat. In order to adequately assess the vulnerability of our respective communities, however, we require data that has enough granularity to a) pinpoint high risk areas and b) correlate that vulnerability with health outcomes. With this hyperlocal data provided by our technical partners, we can then direct decisionmakers to implement projects, codes, standards, regulations, and policies that protect our populations from extreme heat."
(The detailed methodology for obtaining hyperlocal data and conducting the vulnerability analysis could be repurposed for Task 3)
Partnership Development (for the Large Grant Application section on engaging public entities): 
"As COMMON evolves, we fully intend to partner with tribes and local public authorities, as well as government agencies and public landowners. Though this phase of COMMON's evolution does not propose naturebased solutions on public property, we hope that in the future expansion of the Observation Network, we can secure public cooperation on a number of large implementation projects installed on public rightofways. These include installing green corridors along public transportation routes, 'green oases' protecting mobile home parks populated by farmworkers, and 'cool groves' that provide shade in agricultural hotspots."
CoBenefits (for the Projects with Multiple Benefits section):
"The proposed extreme heat monitoring and mitigation network offers a multifaceted approach to addressing the challenges posed by climate change, particularly for vulnerable communities. By generating hyperlocal predictions of biophysical heat stress and providing a userfriendly app, the project empowers community members to navigate extreme heat events safely. The installation of thermal stress sensors in neighborhoods and the provision of mobile sensors to farmworkers and other atrisk populations, when combined with health data from UCM will generate valuable data to better understand the impact of extreme heat on public health outcomes and healthcare costs."
These excerpts provide a strong starting point for the Fresno RFP narrative. The overall vision, communitydriven approach, technical methodologies, and emphasis on cobenefits articulated in the CAMO proposal are highly relevant and transferable. Some tailoring will be needed to adapt the specifics to the Fresno context and RFP requirements, but the core elements can be effectively repurposed.

  - Brent Bucknum (BB): Technical overview and innovative approach
  - Ivan Heitmann (IH): Project Manager, to address technical and management questions
  - Daniel Fleischer (DF): Technical expert for detailed questions
  - Mona: Local arborist, experience with Fresno COG projects, and deeply networked in the small towns and cities of the proposed project.

  - Ricardo (Binational): Community engagement lead (could not attend)
Key Interview Strategy Points:
1.  Technical Presentation (Brent)
  - BB to provide initial overview highlighting dual capabilities:
      - Innovative heat mapping/monitoring/modeling
      - Pedestrian-level greening design
  - Team's advantage: Can do both mapping and design, unlike competitors who typically specialize in one or the other
  - Experience with civil engineers, multimodal transit, and traffic engineering
  - Examples from Richmond, Dallas, and Oakland projects
2.  Project Management (Ivan)
  - IH to address deeper technical questions and project management details
  - Focus on Working Group structure and management
  - Highlight experience managing similar budgets and projects (MTCA, Prescott, STEP, CARB)
  - Emphasize experience with similar authorities (Dallas COG project)
3.  Community Engagement
  - Binational committed to 10,000 surveys through existing programs
  - Innovative approach: Using existing events (tax prep, medical signups, etc.) to gather lived experience
  - SH's role refined to:
      - Reviewing/refining community engagement plan
      - Coordinating with city agencies
      - Ensuring alignment with COG goals
      - Supporting agency coordination
4.  Local Knowledge & Experience
  - Mona, Sheila, and Binational all have deep expertise in Central Valley 
  - Mona understands its vegetation, conditions, and how trees can address heat-related challenges
  - Existing relationships with target communities
  - Understanding of local conditions and requirements
  - Previous work with Fresno COG projects
Action Items:
1.  BB to select 5-6 representative slides from submission
2.  Have additional slides ready for detailed questions
3.  Have Felt board prepared for meeting
4.  MP to check with Binational before morning meeting
Important Context:
  - COG values community engagement more than initially assumed
  - Technical capability important for leveraging future infrastructure funding
  - Team should emphasize both technical expertise and community connections
  - Local knowledge provides competitive advantage
  - Need to demonstrate ability to execute within budget

Our assumption had been that COG will have fewer questions about community outreach and more on tech and transit, and that Sheila and Mona could help with that

      - What is my role exactly

## 11/25/24 Hyphae x Binational
### **Meeting Notes: Fresno Heat Grant with BOCC (11/25/24)**
**Attendees:**
  - Brent Bucknum (Hyphae Design Lab)
  - Maxwell Pingeon (Hyphae Design Lab)
  - Ricardo Castorena (BOCC, President/CEO)
  - Rico Peralta (BOCC, Sr. Director of Impact & Partnerships)
-----
**Key Themes and Discussions**
BOCC’s Unique Approach to Community Engagement
1.  **Culturally Resonant Outreach:**
      - BOCC integrates community engagement into culturally significant events, including:
          - Toys for Tots (serving 4,000 people annually).
          - Free tax preparation services, which engage attendees for 1.5–2 hours.
          - Health screenings and food distribution drives, leveraging established relationships to collect actionable data.
          - Events like **Dia de los Reyes (Three Kings Day)**, spring festivals, and resource fairs.
2.  **Trusted Messenger Model:**
      - BOCC emphasizes trust and familiarity. Residents recognize BOCC staff, such as Rico and Ricardo, as trusted partners, creating a more authentic connection.
      - Partnerships with organizations supporting Oaxacan and Punjabi communities ensure inclusivity across linguistic and cultural barriers.
3.  **Survey Scalability and Efficiency:**
      - Successfully conducted 18,000 surveys in three months for California Jobs First.
      - Average 150 surveys during health screening events and up to 1,300 surveys in six weeks.
4.  **Data Collection During Events:**
      - Surveys are embedded into event activities (e.g., an exit survey for tax preparation or food distribution).
      - Feedback is gathered through informal methods, such as mapping exercises and quick conversations, tailored to the audience's preferences.
-----
**Challenges with Conventional Outreach**
1.  **Low Turnout at Public Meetings:**
      - Standard community meetings typically attract only 3–5 attendees, often repeat participants with narrow perspectives.
      - BOCC’s strategy avoids these pitfalls by embedding engagement in events that are already meaningful to the community.
2.  **Barriers to Participation:**
      - Meetings held during working hours or without added incentives are inaccessible to many residents.
      - BOCC addresses these barriers with family-friendly scheduling (e.g., weekend afternoons) and incentives such as meals, gift cards, and raffles.
-----
**Alignment with the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan**
1.  **Role in Project Execution:**
      - **Community Feedback:** BOCC will collect input on heat’s impact on mobility, gathering patterns, and daily life, feeding actionable insights into the technical design process.
      - **Translation Services:** BOCC will provide on-site interpretation and translation of materials during working group sessions and community meetings.
      - **Leveraging Existing Events:** BOCC’s embedded engagement model ensures seamless integration of project goals into their event calendar.
2.  **Geographic and Community Coverage:**
      - BOCC’s work spans key underserved areas identified for this project, including Tranquillity, Lanare, Huron, Coalinga, and Riverdale.
      - Their established event ecosystem already operates in communities highlighted for their lack of infrastructure, such as inadequate cooling centers.
3.  **Synergies with Other Projects:**
      - BOCC’s current initiatives (e.g., Rural Mobile Health screenings) align naturally with the Extreme Heat Plan’s objectives, allowing for efficient resource sharing and coordination.
-----
**Project Deliverables and Next Steps**
1.  **Deliverables:**
      - Translation services for meetings and materials.
      - Comprehensive survey data contextualized with testimonials and visual documentation.
      - Event integration to maximize outreach and efficiency.
2.  **Next Steps:**
      - BOCC to outline a schedule of existing events where project outreach can be embedded.
      - Hyphae to finalize the budget and confirm deliverables aligned with BOCC’s engagement scope.
      - Draft engagement plan to incorporate BOCC’s trusted messenger model and linguistic inclusivity.
-----
**Action Items**
1.  **BOCC:**
      - Submit a list of key target events and timelines for embedding project outreach.
      - Provide cost estimates for translation services and incentives to increase participation.
2.  **Hyphae:**
      - Incorporate BOCC’s engagement model into the project proposal, emphasizing their unique ability to surpass standard outreach methods.
      - Coordinate working group formation with input from BOCC’s community network.
3.  **Follow-Up Meeting:**
      - Schedule follow-up to refine roles and finalize budget allocations.
### Blurb \#1:
**Rico Peralta and Ricardo Castorena’s Role in the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan**
**Experience and Expertise  
**Rico Peralta, Sr. Director of Impact & Partnerships, and Ricardo Castorena, President and CEO of the Binational of Central California (BOCC), bring decades of collective experience in grassroots community engagement and program delivery across the Central Valley. BOCC has established itself as a trusted community partner, with a proven track record of mobilizing diverse populations through culturally resonant events and initiatives.
Rico and Ricardo have spearheaded numerous impactful programs that reflect BOCC’s deep connections within the region:
  - Organizing the largest **Toys for Tots event** in the Central Valley, serving over 4,000 people annually.
  - Integrating engagement activities into **free tax preparation services**, which keep attendees on-site for extended periods, allowing for meaningful data collection and conversation.
  - Hosting community-driven events like health screenings, food distribution drives, and culturally significant celebrations, such as **Dia de los Reyes (Three Kings Day)**.
Their expertise extends to managing surveys at scale—BOCC has conducted over 18,000 surveys in just three months for California Jobs First. These efforts ensure actionable data, critical for informing community-driven projects. Additionally, BOCC excels in engaging linguistically and culturally diverse populations, forging partnerships with organizations serving Oaxacan and Punjabi communities to address linguistic and cultural barriers.
**Role in the Project  
**BOCC will play a central role in ensuring the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan is grounded in genuine community input and participation.
  - **Working Group Formation and Engagement:** Leveraging BOCC’s extensive network of relationships with community-based organizations (CBOs), local leaders, and residents, Rico and Ricardo will support the formation and operation of the project’s working group. They will provide insight into key stakeholders and help drive participation from hard-to-reach populations.
  - **Community Outreach Integration:** BOCC will embed surveys and data collection efforts into its established event ecosystem, including food distributions, health screenings, and resource fairs. This approach will ensure engagement reaches populations most affected by extreme heat while minimizing barriers to participation.
  - **Trusted Messenger Model:** As recognized and trusted figures in the community, Rico and Ricardo will ensure that outreach efforts resonate authentically with local residents, fostering trust and maximizing the relevance of the collected input.
**Value Added to the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan  
**BOCC’s dynamic engagement model, paired with its deep-rooted presence in Fresno County, ensures a level of inclusivity and efficiency that surpasses conventional methods. Their ability to simultaneously deliver resources and gather community input makes them indispensable to the project. With BOCC’s leadership, the plan will be informed by the voices of those who are directly impacted, ensuring that proposed solutions are equitable, actionable, and community-driven.
**Management Approach  
**Rico and Ricardo will coordinate closely with Hyphae Design Lab and Fresno COG to align their outreach efforts with project milestones. They will oversee the implementation of surveys, facilitate data collection at key events, and provide comprehensive reporting, including testimonials and visual documentation, to inform technical and planning decisions. Their approach ensures that engagement is not only effective but also a seamless extension of the services BOCC already provides to the community.
### Blurb \# 2
**BOCC’s Distinctive Role in Community Engagement for the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan**
The Binational of Central California (BOCC) offers an unmatched approach to community engagement, rooted in its deep integration with Central Valley communities and its ability to embed outreach efforts within culturally relevant events. Unlike conventional outreach consultants, BOCC avoids sterile public meetings with low turnout, instead harnessing the power of trusted relationships and high-participation events. This innovative model ensures meaningful, actionable engagement while addressing the unique needs of Fresno County’s diverse populations.
**Reaching the Community Where They Already Gather  
**BOCC’s strategy centers on embedding surveys and discussions into activities that already draw large crowds, such as:
  - **Health screenings** conducted in collaboration with the Rural Mobile Health Unit, which average 150 screenings per session and yielded 1,300 completed surveys in just six weeks.
  - **Free tax preparation sessions** that naturally engage attendees for extended periods, providing ample opportunity for data collection and dialogue.
  - Community events like the **Toys for Tots giveaway**, which serves 4,000 individuals, or the **backpack campaign** and spring festivals, ensuring outreach to families with children.  
    By aligning engagement with these events, BOCC maximizes participation while minimizing the burden on community members, fostering trust and inclusivity.
**Tailored Outreach for Equity and Access  
**BOCC excels in bridging gaps for underserved communities, particularly those with linguistic and cultural barriers. Their partnerships extend to organizations serving Oaxacan indigenous language speakers and the Punjabi community, ensuring representation for groups traditionally excluded from outreach efforts. Events are designed with accessibility in mind, incorporating resources like:
  - Medi-Cal registration
  - Appointments with the Mexican consulate
  - Meals and family activities such as volleyball tournaments, soccer games, and the unique “taco throwdown.”
**From Insights to Actionable Data  
**BOCC’s engagement model not only gathers data but transforms it into actionable insights. For example, by leveraging their robust networks, they conducted over 18,000 surveys in just three months for California Jobs First—demonstrating their efficiency and capacity. These insights inform targeted interventions, such as identifying hotspots like Tranquillity, Lanare, Huron, and Riverdale, which lack critical infrastructure like cooling centers despite being severely affected by extreme heat.
**Embedding Environmental Equity into the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan  
**BOCC’s trusted presence within these communities ensures that outreach efforts reach the right people—the individuals living and working in these spaces who understand their challenges best. By incorporating these voices, BOCC contributes to a planning process that respects local expertise and drives equitable outcomes. Their ability to weave outreach into the fabric of community life guarantees higher turnout and more meaningful participation than standard approaches could achieve.
**Deliverables and Value Added  
**BOCC provides more than just engagement. They deliver:
  - Comprehensive survey data contextualized by video testimonials and photographs to demonstrate real community impact.
  - Feedback sessions designed for flexibility, from interactive map-based exercises to online surveys, tailored to the community’s preferences.
  - A scalable model for engagement that ensures Fresno COG meets its outreach objectives efficiently and inclusively.
Through BOCC’s collaboration, Hyphae Design Lab can anchor the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan in true community-led priorities, ensuring a plan driven by equity, trust, and local expertise.
### Blurb \#3:
**BOCC’s Distinctive Role in Community Engagement for the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan**
The Binational of Central California (BOCC) offers an unmatched approach to community engagement, rooted in its deep integration with Central Valley communities and its ability to embed outreach efforts within culturally relevant events. Unlike conventional outreach consultants, BOCC avoids sterile public meetings with low turnout, instead harnessing the power of trusted relationships and high-participation events. This innovative model ensures meaningful, actionable engagement while addressing the unique needs of Fresno County’s diverse populations.
**Reaching the Community Where They Already Gather**
BOCC’s strategy centers on embedding surveys and discussions into activities that already draw large crowds, such as:
  - **Health screenings** conducted in collaboration with the Rural Mobile Health Unit, which average 150 screenings per session and yielded 1,300 completed surveys in just six weeks.
  - **Free tax preparation sessions** that naturally engage attendees for extended periods, providing ample opportunity for data collection and dialogue.
  - Community events like the **Toys for Tots giveaway**, which serves 4,000 individuals, or the **backpack campaign** and spring festivals, ensuring outreach to families with children.
By aligning engagement with these events, BOCC maximizes participation while minimizing the burden on community members, fostering trust and inclusivity.

### Blurb \#4 
**Rico Peralta and Ricardo Castorena’s Role in the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan**
**Experience and Expertise  
**Rico Peralta, Sr. Director of Impact & Partnerships, and Ricardo Castorena, President and CEO of the Binational of Central California (BOCC), bring unparalleled expertise in culturally responsive community engagement across eight counties in the Central Valley, including Fresno. With a focus on reaching California’s most vulnerable populations—Latinos, Indigenous farmworkers, non-English speakers, and rural residents with limited broadband access—BOCC has established itself as a trusted messenger in marginalized communities.
BOCC’s impact is rooted in its ability to connect with hard-to-reach populations through culturally resonant and event-driven outreach. Signature initiatives include:
  - Hosting **Toys for Tots**, the largest such event in the Valley, serving over 4,000 attendees annually.
  - Conducting over **18,000 surveys in three months** during campaigns like California Jobs First, leveraging trusted relationships and culturally appropriate messaging.
  - Reaching 300,000 residents through targeted outreach, including text campaigns, resource fairs, and door-to-door engagement.
  - Embedding disaster preparedness and resilience education into over **40 annual events**, including health screenings, tax preparation services, and culturally significant celebrations.
BOCC’s track record demonstrates their ability to engage diverse communities through multiple channels, including bilingual and multilingual outreach in Spanish, Hmong, Punjabi, and Indigenous languages like Mixteco and Triqui.
**Role in the Project  
**BOCC’s involvement in the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan will extend far beyond traditional outreach goals, ensuring authentic engagement and actionable community input.
  - **Data-Driven Outreach:** BOCC will embed surveys, map-based exercises, and quick-feedback tools into its existing event framework to collect hyperlocal data on heat impacts, mobility, and infrastructure needs.
  - **Translation Services:** BOCC will provide on-site interpretation and translated materials in the region’s most spoken languages to ensure inclusivity.
  - **Community-Driven Strategies:** Trusted messengers like Rico and Ricardo will lead peer-to-peer engagement, ensuring that outreach resonates with the lived experiences of Fresno County’s diverse populations.
  - **Comprehensive Reporting:** BOCC will deliver detailed engagement metrics, testimonials, and visual documentation to inform technical and planning decisions.
**Value Added to the Fresno Extreme Heat Plan  
**BOCC’s event-driven model of engagement ensures participation levels that far exceed standard community outreach efforts. Their ability to seamlessly integrate technical engagement into culturally relevant events, paired with their expertise in data collection and peer-to-peer communication, makes them indispensable to the project. BOCC’s alignment with Hyphae’s mission ensures a plan that is both equitable and actionable, informed by the lived experiences of those most affected by extreme heat.
**Deliverables**
1.  Translation services and multilingual outreach materials.
2.  Embedded engagement at events like Toys for Tots, tax preparation sessions, and health fairs.
3.  Surveys, focus groups, and community feedback integrated into actionable insights for the plan.
4.  Comprehensive reports including visual documentation, metrics, and testimonials.
